Sinner parts ways with coach Cahill after five-year run

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Jannik Sinner has confirmed the end of his hugely successful five-year partnership with coach Darren Cahill with Magnus Norman ‌poised to replace him; You can watch the ATP Paris Masters live on Sky Sports with the WTA Finals in Indian Wells starting on Sunday

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Jannik Sinner has parted ways with coach Darren Cahill with Magnus Norman poised to replace the Australian.

Sinner has won five Grand Slam titles and spent 95 weeks as world No 1 under the guidance of the veteran Australian, who worked alongside Italian Simone Vagnozzi, but will team up with Swede Magnus Norman in 2027.

Cahill had planned to retire at the end of last season but the pair made a bet that, if Sinner won the Wimbledon title, he could decide on the 61-year-old's future, and he stayed on for another year.

Sinner, who has been sidelined by a knee injury since winning his second Wimbledon crown in July, said: "Darren has been an incredibly important part of my journey. I want to thank Darren for his belief in me, his honesty and his dedication. He has helped me grow both as a player and as a person, and his guidance will stay with me throughout my career."

Former world No 2 Norman ​arrives with experience and a proven record having taken compatriot Robin Soderling to the top five in the ​rankings and two French Open finals before guiding Swiss ‌Stan Wawrinka to three Grand Slam titles.

The 50-year-old Swede will take his place alongside ​coach Simone Vagnozzi, who like Cahill started working with Italian Sinner in 2022.

Alexander Zverev is guaranteed to finish 2026 as ​the year-end world No 1, with Sinner deciding not to play in any of the remaining ATP tournaments ‌this year.

Stream tennis with no contract on NOW and the Sky Sports App. You can watch the ATP Paris Masters live on Sky Sports this week with the WTA Finals in Indian Wells starting on Sunday.

What next for DeChambeau and co? - LIV Golf Q&A

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

What are the major questions surrounding LIV Golf following its dissolution this week? Where did it all go wrong for the breakaway league? What next for it's star players, like Bryson DeChambeau? And is there a pathway back for them on the PGA Tour or DP World Tour?

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

LIV Golf appears to be over, with it announced this week that it will enter liquidation.

But how has it got to this point for the breakaway league that threw huge sums of money at the sport and played the role of major disruptor when it entered onto the scene five years ago.

What now for the big-name players that haven't yet announced their departure ahead of the ultimately doomed LIV 2.0? And what next for LIV itself?

LIV Golf's attempt to establish itself as a permanent rival to the PGA Tour has effectively come to an end as, on November 5, the league confirmed it would begin an orderly wind-down of its operations after failing to secure the financial backing needed to continue.

"The writing was on the wall from April of this year, when the Saudi Public Investment Fund [PIF] announced they'd be pulling the plug, that they wouldn't be investing in the tour any longer," Sky Sports News' Jamie Weir said.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"They came in with the intent to change the face of men's professional golf. £6bn of Saudi money has been pumped into it over the last four years and most of it has gone down the drain.

"You would have to say that, because they haven't made much of an impact - certainly not in terms of viewing figures."

LIV subsequently filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in September, with investment firm BC Partners emerging as a potential new backer, proposing a deal worth around $300m to help launch LIV 2.0.

However, that rescue plan collapsed amid a mass player exodus and the proposed investment was withdrawn. LIV has now moved towards liquidation rather than attempting to launch another season.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Former European Ryder Cup captain Paul McGinley believes LIV Golf was destined to fail from the very start, following the news of the former PIF-backed venture being wound down.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Speaking on RTÉ's Morning Ireland, McGinley said: "I think it was based on bad fundamentals, I think that was the main thing.

"LIV had an idea that team events was going to work in an individual sport that golf is. They also took on the might of America, but as Warren Buffet would say in business terms, 'don't bet against Uncle Sam'.

"They went head-to-head, toe-to-toe with corporate America, which is the backbone of the PGA Tour, and came out on the wrong side of that. They were incredibly strong and able to move the financial needle in order to stand toe-to-toe with the Saudis.

"Ultimately, LIV wasn't thought through well and it wasn't a great idea. It was a flawed business model with really bad advice that the Saudis got in terms of the power that they were taking on."

The Golf Channel's Brandel Chamblee specifically pointed to October 12, and the DP World Tour's announcement that players who signed with LIV 2.0 wouldn't be allowed to play on the tour, as the beginning of the end for LIV.

Chamblee said: "When Guy Kennings [DP World Tour CEO] said what he did, that was the death knell strategically.

"He was saying, 'look, you don't get to have your cake and eat it too'. That is the big, significant moment in in ending LIV Golf.

"We're going to say it's November 5 when LIV died. But really, LIV died on October 12."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

McGinley added to Sky Sports: "To be quite blunt and frank, they [LIV players] filled their pockets with money and had made enough they needed to make and now was an opportunity to come back in, join the ecosystem again, get back on the gravy train of the DP World Tour, along with the PGA Tour, back inside the fold and move forward.

"It's nothing more, nothing less than that.

"When it became about money and 'is this product [LIV 2.0] believable to go forward and make money?' they obviously decided no and in their heart of hearts they knew that was the case."

Bryson DeChambeau has largely remained tight-lipped as LIV Golf's financial troubles have unfolded, even as several of his fellow professionals have begun exploring their options for an exit.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

In May, he did comment on the matter, saying that he would focus on his YouTube channel if LIV Golf failed, picking that over a potential return to the PGA Tour, but there are rumours circling that the two-time US Open champion could be given special privileges to play on the DP World Tour going forward.

Sky Sports' Dame Laura Davies would love to see that happen. "I'm licking my chops. "Love him or hate him, you can't stop watching him.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"If you ever get the chance, watch the guy hit the ball. He's just a little bit different - a little bit like John Daly back in the day when he was different.

"Bryson's different, and so the DP World Tour could be adding a massive asset to their artillery, which is great."

Mickelson was arguably the LIV's most significant signing in its early days. The six-time major champion's decision to join in 2022 was a major statement of intent.

Four years on, his future is one of the biggest question marks off the back of the league's collapse.

At 56 years old, Mickelson's best is long behind him - even when considering his remarkable 2021 PGA Championship success - and, thus far, he has not signalled his intent with regards to any future plans.

His relationship with the PGA Tour has been particularly complicated, given his involvement in the original push for LIV and the legal disputes that followed.

One possibility is that Mickelson focuses only on playing at major championships, where he can still compete through his existing exemptions where applicable.

Further complicating his future in the game, however, are allegations of misconduct made against him, with golf publication Skratch alleged earlier this year that he's is no longer welcome at three California golf clubs because of inappropriate behaviour towards women.

Mickelson denies the allegations, with his representatives calling the piece an "anonymously sourced drive-by shooting".

Mickelson played only one LIV tournament this year, in South Africa in March, missing the rest of their schedule to prioritise "a family health matter".

"This is the great question. What happens to all those players? And it's complicated," Weir said.

"As far as the PGA Tour are concerned, they have been very consistent. They have said you need to serve a one-year suspension from your last LIV event before you're eligible to play in the PGA Tour again.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"That means it won't be until August 2027 that any of these players are eligible to even play on the PGA Tour, as there won't be the same loophole, the same olive branch that was extended in January of this year to four specific players: Brooks Koepka, Bryson DeChambeau, John Ram, and Cameron Smith, which was essentially 'come back and all is forgiven'.

"Koepka was the only one who took that offer. The other three are probably ruing that decision now.

"What that means is that the DP World Tour is now the most desirable option for a lot of these players. They have until November 15 to take up DP World Tour membership, if eligible.

"In the cases of Rahm and Tyrell Hatton, they never gave up their membership. So that's all hunky dory.

"The likes of veterans Lee Westwood and Paul Casey, they haven't stated their intention yet, but they have career money list exemptions, so they could rejoin the DP World Tour.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"Then it gets more complicated. DeChambeau, for example, by winning the US Open in 2024, that got him five years exemption on the DP World Tour. But for every year you don't take up membership, you lose a year off the end.

"Bryson didn't take up membership in 2025 or '26, meaning he's lost his chance to in '28 and '29. He has the option for 2027, but that's his final option.

"For other major champions, the likes of Smith, Phil Mickelson and Dustin Johnson, that door is closed.

"It means they're going to have to rely on invites to events on the DP World Tour, and even if they get invites, they will have to add value to that event. And it's not going to be to the premier events.

"It's going to be a long and very tricky road back to the top."

As things stand, LIV Golf as we know it, is heading for the end, with its assets being prepared for liquidation.

LIV's creditors committee will meet on November 20 and another hearing in the bankruptcy case is scheduled for November 24.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

That does not necessarily mean, however, that the concept of a breakaway golf competition will disappear forever.

Much like the proposed Super League in football in 2021, the threat of another attempted coup could loom large indefinitely.

LIV demonstrated that there was an appetite, among other things, for some sort of team golf style events, a more favourable schedule, more international tournaments and bigger prize funds, which has forced the PGA Tour to reconsider aspects of its own business model.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

There could still be opportunities for investors to purchase LIV's remaining assets, trademarks or other commercial interests.

However, recreating the competition would require significant financial backing and the commitment of leading players. But those burnt by its ultimate collapse on this occasion will surely be reticent to attach themselves to the brand once more.

And without the players, finding investors willing to take on the financial risk will be nigh on impossible.

Russell to take grid penalty at Sao Paulo GP

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Watch Friday's practice sessions from the Sao Paulo Grand Prix at 3.30pm and 7pm live on Sky Sports F1

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

George Russell will take a five-place grid penalty at this weekend's Sao Paulo Grand Prix for use of a fresh Mercedes engine.

The Briton was always expected to take an additional grid penalty before the end of the season after the failure that stopped his car at the Bahrain GP in Malaysia.

He took a fifth engine of the season, and other power unit parts, at last month's Singapore GP, which dropped him to the back of the field there.

He will now take another fresh internal combustion engine at Interlagos, although this time that only triggers a five-place demotion. He will therefore start Sunday's race no higher than sixth on Sunday's race grid.

Mercedes have confirmed there is no Additional Development and Upgrade Opportunities (ADUO) element to the change, with the world championship leaders yet to introduce their one allowed upgrade this season.

Confirming the news of his penalty in a video on Mercedes' Instagram Story, Russell said: "I mean we always had to take a grid penalty at some point.

"Obviously we took one in Singapore but from Singapore until the end of the season, we felt this was a good place to take it.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"Obviously, Lewis had a big grid penalty here a number of years ago [2021] and did pretty well.

"Five places doesn't seem that bad and we'll try and come back through."

Interlagos is a circuit on which overtaking is favourable, making this venue a logical one to take a grid drop. Rain also threatens the race weekend.

Russell's championship challenge officially ended last Sunday in Mexico when team-mate Kimi Antonelli clinched 2026's drivers' title. Russell has slipped to fourth in the standings with four races to go this season.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Dubois fights Han in three-belt unification on February 20 live on Sky

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Caroline Dubois will take on Stephanie Han in Texas on February 20 live on Sky Sports in UK prime time; Dubois and Han will fight to unify the WBC, WBO and WBA lightweight world titles; "I'm coming to take her title and show everyone why I'm the best lightweight in the world," Dubois said

Caroline Dubois will box rival champion Stephanie Han on February 20 in Texas live on Sky Sports in UK prime time.

The unbeaten champions will fight at GECU Arena at the Don Haskins Center in El Paso with three world titles on the line as London's Dubois travels to fight Han in front of her hometown fans.

Most Valuable Promotion's unified WBC and WBO lightweight world champion, Dubois is one of the most accomplished young talents in women's boxing.

"I can't wait to get back in the ring and I can't wait to go into Stephanie Han's hometown and add another world title to my collection," Dubois said.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

"I respect what she's accomplished, but I'm coming to El Paso to take her title and show everyone why I'm the best lightweight in the world.

"These are the kinds of fights I've been working toward my entire career. I want all the belts, I want the biggest fights, and I want to prove myself against the best. On Saturday, February 20, I'm coming to make a statement and take another step toward becoming undisputed.

"A big thank you to MVP and Sky for making this fight happen and continuing to give women's boxing the platform it deserves."

The southpaw known as "Sweet Caroline" enjoyed a decorated amateur career that included gold at the 2018 Youth Olympic Games, four European Youth Championships and representing Great Britain at the Tokyo Olympics.

Turning professional in 2022, Dubois quickly established herself as a dominant force in the lightweight division, capturing the WBC world title before unifying the WBC and WBO championships with a victory over Terri Harper at MVPW-01 in April.

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

She most recently defended her titles against Amelia Moore in August and now looks to add the WBA championship to her collection in the biggest unification fight of her professional career.

Han is an El Paso native, a devoted mother of two, veteran of the El Paso Police Department and former beauty pageant competitor whose remarkable journey has made her one of the most compelling figures in women's boxing.

Known as "The Enforcer," Han captured the vacant WBA lightweight world title with a first-round knockout of Hannah Terlep in 2025 and has since successfully defended her championship three times, including back-to-back victories over combat sports legend Holly Holm.

Most recently Han defeated Holm in front of her hometown fans in El Paso in May, further cementing her status as one of the division's elite champions.

She will now face the biggest challenge of her career at GECU Arena at the Don Haskins Center, with an opportunity to unify three major world titles.

Han said: "This fight is a dream come true! Finally, I have the opportunity to unify titles in my division and get one step closer to becoming the undisputed queen of the lightweight division.

"To be able to fight at home at GECU Arena at the Don Haskins Center is incredibly special, and I'm grateful to MVP for their support to make this happen. I invite all of El Paso to come out and support on Saturday, February 20 and witness this moment."

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Nakisa Bidarian, CEO of Most Valuable Promotions, said: "Caroline Dubois and Stephanie Han are two undefeated world champions at the top of one of the deepest and most competitive divisions in boxing.

"Stephanie's story is remarkable. She's a police officer, a mother, a former beauty queen and a world champion who has become a source of pride for El Paso.

"Caroline has established herself as one of the most talented young champions in the sport, and bringing these two women together with three major world titles on the line is a huge moment for MVP and women's boxing.

"El Paso has shown up for its hometown hero time and time again, so we owed it to this city to bring one of the biggest fights in boxing to GECU Arena at the Don Haskins Center on Saturday, February 20, live in UK prime time on Sky Sports."

Lampard looks for Coventry progress as belief grows

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Coventry lost their opening four Premier League games by an aggregate of 10-0

"I would like to think there is a higher ceiling," says Frank Lampard.

After four opening defeats without scoring, a run which had Coventry written off, the Sky Blues are finding their feet.

They could move up to 11th in the Premier League with victory at Everton on Friday as Lampard returns to the Toffees, who he managed for a year from January 2022.

A win would leave them on 12 points, level with eighth-placed Bournemouth, a quarter of the way through their first season back in the Premier League in 25 years.

Just Middlesbrough (1996-97), Burnley (2009-10) and Cardiff (2013-14) have been relegated from the Premier League after taking 12 points from 10 games.

History is on Coventry's side as last season's Championship winners look to prove the doubters wrong.

Coventry fan impressed by Lampard's Premier League changes

Despite Coventry's start to the season - after defeats by Arsenal, Hull, Manchester City and Brighton - the optimism at the training ground did not waver.

The positive mood at the club's Ryton base remains, following Saturday's victory over Sunderland, as it did after the 5-0 home thumping by Brighton.

It was accepted the game, where Coventry played almost 45 minutes with 10 men following Taiwo Awoniyi's dismissal, can happen in the Premier League - and there was no hangover.

There was a belief there was enough evidence in performances against Hull, Manchester City and Arsenal the Sky Blues could compete in the top flight.

Then, after a 3-1 Carabao Cup defeat by Aston Villa, Lampard tweaked the system to match Nottingham Forest's back three to earn a 1-0 win at the City Ground.

"The result sometimes you can't quite control, but you can control your idea going into a game, that maximises our strengths and looks at the opposition's," said Lampard, who is in before 7.30am every morning, using the gym before training.

"There can be various reasons why we do it. We went to Nottingham Forest and we matched up their formation because I wasn't keen to go there in the moment we were in with a back four and the problems they can give you with wing-backs and 10s.

"It's not just my, or our, choice on formation which is going to win or lose games. We know we have a big fight so every small percentage in our set-up if we feel that way we will do it - but we must feel it's going to be the right one."

Lampard has always been tactically flexible and three wins in five games has allowed Coventry to climb out of the relegation zone.

The stats provide interesting reading too, especially when compared with the 1-0 defeat by Hull which could have been so damaging in the first home game of the season.

Then, Coventry had 68.5% possession but that dropped to 42.6% in the 3-1 win over Sunderland. The Sky Blues also made more than 200 fewer passes and more than 50 fewer forward passes against the Black Cats.

Their passes in the final third more than halved from 128 to just 60, as the patience - and even pragmatism - paid off as the Sky Blues begin to understand the demands of the division.

The benching of skipper Matt Grimes, key in last season's promotion, was also a big call from Lampard but one which has been met with understanding from within.

Coventry still have the lowest xG (expected goals) in the Premier League of 8.34 but lessons have been learned from the frustration of the opening weeks, even if those at the club recognise the difficult start given three of their opening four away games were at Manchester City, Arsenal and Tottenham.

After Everton, Coventry host Crystal Palace and Ipswich either side of a trip to Leeds to complete a run sources felt would be crucial to their survival hopes.

"When you're in the zone, enjoyment of the win is pretty short-lived - you switch your attention to the next game. Relaxation and complacency is the biggest danger in this game," said Lampard.

"If we can keep the strength of feeling, even when difficult results come, we will give ourselves a chance."

Jack Rudoni has attracted admirers after his start to the season

One Premier League manager has privately said if he were to take any players from Coventry, one of them would be Jack Rudoni.

The former Huddersfield midfielder - playing in League One four years ago - has been one of the quickest players to adapt for Coventry, with four goals in 10 appearances in all competitions, in a group short of top-flight experience.

Awoniyi (90 games) and Ethan Pinnock (117) have played the most with just Loum Tchaouna, Ellis Simms, Matt Grimes, Kaine Kesler-Hayden, Dan Bentley and Gus Hamer having featured in the Premier League.

Even then, Simms only made 12 appearances for Everton, Grimes just four for Swansea, Bentley 10 for Wolves and Kesler-Hayden had only played five minutes.

Tchaouna's and Hamer's experiences were relegation with Burnley and Sheffield United.

Lampard knew they would need time to find the grit to stay in the top flight but the Sky Blues have given themselves the chance.

"Sometimes you go through hundreds of games to find that resilience and a lot of our players haven't had that," added Lampard.

"There have been some complications. The squad and ourselves have managed pretty well when you see what we're trying to do."

Has Anderson lived up to the hype after Man City move?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Elliot Anderson started his career at Newcastle before moves to Nottingham Forest and Manchester City

Elliot Anderson's stock has risen astronomically and there were considerable expectations on the midfielder's shoulders after sealing a Â£116m move from Nottingham Forest to Manchester City in the summer.

The 24-year-old established himself as one of the best players in the Premier League and has become a key man for England, helping Thomas Tuchel's side to a third-placed finish at this summer's World Cup.

His highly impressive performances for Forest over the past two seasons had him tracked by the likes of Manchester United and Arsenal before his move to Etihad Stadium.

European football expert Guillem Balague said on BBC Match of the Day he had spoken to City manager Enzo Maresca in the summer and asked if he wants to sign Bournemouth's Alex Scott and Crystal Palace's Adam Wharton.

The Italian's response was "no", adding: "Anderson is going to become the key player for England, not just City."

Anderson may well be afforded a generous reception when he makes his return to the City Ground on Saturday, with Forest supporter Ellie Molloson telling BBC Sport: "I know the stadium will be ringing with 'Geordie Maradona'.

"It will be bittersweet because we miss him, but we are quite proud of just how fantastic a player he is."

Few Forest fans will "begrudge him for his whopping move", but is Anderson managing to live up to the hype in the early days of his City career?

Anderson was one of the standout central midfielders in the Premier League last season.

He topped the rankings for a number of metrics such as touches of the ball, winning possession, contesting duels and drawing the most fouls.

His influence was striking despite featuring in a struggling Forest side which had four different managers and finished only five points above the relegation zone.

The former Newcastle player's tireless work-rate means he has effectively replaced Bernardo Silva and Rodri at City, making the step up to a side that won two trophies last season and could challenge for the title again.

Though Anderson has not fully replicated the outright league-leading dominance he showcased across several metrics last season, his underlying numbers remain elite.

He has drawn more fouls (28) than any other player in the division, while he ranks second for duels won (66) and third for successful passes (610) and touches on the ball (851).

Only Tottenham's Jan Paul van Hecke and Arsenal's Declan Rice have managed more successful passes in the opposition half and line-breaking passes than Anderson, but the City midfielder has played at least 66 minutes fewer than both.

Anderson's deep-lying number six role means he has only one goal and two assists in 14 games this season, though he did lay on Erling Haaland's winner against RB Leipzig in the Champions League on Wednesday.

"He has been amazing," said Haaland afterwards. "He has had an amazing start to his City career and that is what you expect. You have to come straight in and perform.

"It is not easy to come in, you need to adapt and new players and a new coach, so he he has been impressive. Respect."

Elliot Anderson a 'potential legend' at Man City

In Tuesday's training session open to the media before City's trip to Leipzig, some players came out looking relaxed in groups but Anderson emerged by himself, doing kick-ups with a laser focus on the ball.

It perhaps hints at a steely determination to remain fixated and give his best at every opportunity, whether that be in training or during the intensity of a match.

Anderson was the first to step up in one of the group drills and was deemed the winner during a set-up which involved a slalom with the ball before smashing a finish into a mini net.

Asked if he was surprised at how quickly Anderson had settled in to life at City, Maresca told BBC Sport: "Knowing him now, he is a guy who wants to learn every day and we sat with him with video [analysis].

"He wants to understand things, he wants to learn and improve and for a manager it's a dream because you know that he wants to learn and you can do different things."

Molloson adds: "The way Anderson reads the game, the way he dominates midfield, he is such an intelligent football player and he is so young, there is still so much more to come from him.

"He is a leader on the pitch and I just think he is phenomenal. I think Forest fans would definitely have him back in a heartbeat."

Maresca has acknowledged Anderson's box-to-box qualities but sees him as City's number six this season. The demands of this role differ from how Anderson has previously been used.

Following Manchester City's 4â1 win over Crystal Palace in August, Anderson said: "I'm loving [my role but] it's a bit different to what I've been used to. I've got to be a bit more structured, that's what I'm learning, to stay in my position. At Forest I was doing a little bit more."

His physicality, intensity and intelligence make him an excellent front-footed midfielder, capable of pressing players high or wide, winning the ball and starting attacks. When City have pressed man-to-man but been played through, Anderson has often impressively stopped attacks alone.

Brighton launch a dangerous counter-attack but Anderson impressively wins the ball back

Anderson's value in a more expansive role was seen in his assist for Haaland's winner at Leipzig this week, as he drove with the ball before playing a cross, reminiscent of Kevin de Bruyne.

Although City's holding midfielder, Anderson's skillset makes him effective across the pitch

The same qualities that Anderson posesses have also been exploited at times.

Habitually, he reacts to players more than thinking about positions, as he is able to win the ball back more often than not from most players. As a result, some opponents have looked to position themselves near him before moving away, enticing the Englishman to follow.

Sunderland's Enzo le Fee made constant off-the-ball runs around and beyond Anderson in September. The England midefielder, almost magnetically, locked on to Le Fee, tracking those runs, which left space in front of City's defence.

Paris St-Germain and Brighton also used these decoy runs to pull Anderson away, creating space in midfield.

Here is an example of Le Fee using Anderson's front-footedness against him. By dropping deep and wide, Le Fee entices Anderson to follow him, vacating his holding midfield position

This opens up space in the centre of the pitch for Sunderland to find their striker, Brian Brobbey, in one pass

Ayyoub Bouaddi has been trialled alongside Anderson in midfield. When Anderson does vacate the holding midfield position to press, something we saw against PSG, a second defensive midfielder alongside him can help protect the centre of the pitch â a temporary fix as Anderson moulds his impressive skillset to Maresca's asks.

In 2021, Guardiola reflected on Rodri's first season, saying: "Last season he moved too much. The holding midfielder has to be there, don't move, be there." In 2024, he won the Ballon d'Or.

City will certainly be pleased with the quality of Anderson so far. It would also be fair to say his debut season has begun better than Rodri's did in 2020. But both City and Anderson will be hoping for a development in positional understanding that mimics the player he was brought in to replace.

Newcastle's Dedic stopped by police after being spotted with bow and arrows

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Amar Dedic has featured in seven Premier League games since joining Newcastle in August

Newcastle United defender Amar Dedic was stopped by police after being spotted carrying a bow and arrows at a Northumberland beauty spot.

Bosnia-Herzegovina international Dedic, 24, was with a friend when he was stopped in a car after a concerned member of the public raised the alarm.

Reports say six police cars, external were dispatched, including armed police, and that Dedic and his companion were stopped about seven miles away from where they were originally spotted.

Police searched the car and seized the bow and arrows but were satisfied the two men were not a threat to the public.

BBC Sport has been told Dedic had no ill intention and has fully co-operated with police.

It is reported Newcastle goalkeeper Mark Gillespie, 34, and former defender Paul Dummett, 35, arrived at the scene while police were speaking to Dedic.

It is understood the pair were in the area by chance and stopped to offer assistance.

The incident happened on Wednesday, two days after Dedic - who joined from Benfica for a fee in the region of Â£30m in August - had played in Newcastle's 1-0 home defeat by Everton.

Newcastle, who are 13th in the Premier League table on 11 points from nine games, have not commented.

Northumbria Police has released a statement and said enquiries are ongoing. The force has not named Dedic.

It said it received reports of two men carrying a bow and arrows while walking along a track by the reservoir on Military Road in the Harlow Hill area.

"Officers attended and subsequently stopped a vehicle in the Darras Hall area and seized a bow and arrows," added the statement.

"At this stage, it is understood they were for recreational use and there was no threat to the public."

Newcastle are at Fulham on Saturday (15:00 GMT) before the November international break.

Dedic, who has featured in 10 league and cup games since joining Newcastle, has been named in the Bosnia-Herzegovina squad for Nations League matches away to Sweden on 14 November and at home to Romania on 17 November.

Palmer misses too many chances to impress - Tuchel

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Thomas Tuchel has named three uncapped players in his England squad

England manager Thomas Tuchel said Cole Palmer "misses too many chances to impress" after not selecting the Chelsea forward for November's Nations League fixtures.

Palmer, 24, dropped out of England's squad for their previous games in early autumn and is absent again for matches against Croatia at Wembley on Thursday, 12 November and Spain in Madrid on Sunday, 15 November.

Tuchel has instead handed Fulham's Josh King and Borussia Dortmund's Jobe Bellingham their first England call-ups. Both players are attacking midfielders.

"We have enough number 10s and we stick to the guys who were in the previous camp because we were happy with the two and a half weeks we had together," said Tuchel.

"We have Josh King as a new player in there, but I want to see him on both wings. He is kind of an extra player and he can play all four positions.

"I guess everyone is desperate to play for England and Cole is no exception.

"We are speaking now about a player that is not in camp. It counts when you are in camp and I just said last time that he misses too many opportunities to impress."

Jobe, 21, joins his brother Jude in the England squad - and they could be the first pair of siblings to feature in an England line-up since Gary and Phil Neville.

England sit second behind Spain in group A3 of the Nations League with three wins and a loss.

One more point from their remaining two matches will secure a place in the two-legged quarter-finals in March, with the winners of those ties advancing to the Nations League Finals in June.

Catch-up with all Thomas Tuchel's responses at his squad selection news conference

Goalkeepers: Jordan Pickford (Everton), Jason Steele (Brighton & Hove Albion), James Trafford (Leeds United)

Defenders: Trent Alexander-Arnold (Real Madrid), Jarrad Branthwaite (Everton), Marc Guehi (Manchester City), Lewis Hall (Newcastle United), Ezri Konsa (Arsenal), Jarell Quansah (Bayer Leverkusen), Djed Spence (Inter Milan), Ben White (Arsenal)

Midfielders: Elliot Anderson (Manchester City), Jobe Bellingham (Borussia Dortmund), Jude Bellingham (Real Madrid), James Garner (Everton), Josh King (Fulham), Myles Lewis-Skelly (Arsenal), Declan Rice (Arsenal)

Forwards: Dominic Calvert-Lewin (Leeds United), Eberechi Eze (Arsenal), Morgan Gibbs-White (Nottingham Forest), Anthony Gordon (Barcelona), Harry Kane (Bayern Munich), Rio Ngumoha (Liverpool), Morgan Rogers (Chelsea)

The selection of Jobe Bellingham for the senior squad means he continues to follow in the footsteps of his older brother.

Both graduates of the Birmingham City academy, the brothers were regular selections in England youth line-ups.

Jobe joined Dortmund from Sunderland in summer 2025 in a deal worth up to Â£31m, having helped the Wearside club win promotion to the Premier League via the play-offs.

The move mirrored one made by Jude, 23, who played for the German side between 2020 and 2023 before signing for Real Madrid.

Tuchel said England Under-21 coach Lee Carsley had been "speaking very highly" of Jobe Bellingham "for a while".

"He is the captain of the under-21s so I think the door should always be open and the pathway should always be open for special performances over a long period of time and that is what Jobe has done," he added.

"He found his way and his rhythm with Dortmund, where he has had an excellent start to the season. He is a regular starter there and now very influential."

Bellingham has been preferred to Kobbie Mainoo, with Tuchel saying the Manchester United midfielder, who was an early withdrawal from the last squad, has had a "difficult spell with his club".

"The door is always open for younger players to overtake and make their mark," he added.

King has had a strong start to the Premier League season, scoring two goals and making one assist, and has played at every youth level for England.

The 19-year-old Fulham academy graduate has scored one goal in nine appearances for England Under-21s, and Tuchel called him "a fantastic character" who "gives us a bit of flexibility".

"We decided to nominate 22 outfield players for flexibility in training and he is the extra player in training, if you will," Tuchel added.

'Palmer misses too many opportunities to impress' - Tuchel

Arsenal forward Bukayo Saka misses out on the November fixtures after sustaining a quadriceps injury against Bayern Munich last month.

The Premier League champions believe the 25-year-old will be absent for six weeks after he was substituted early in the Champions League game against Bayern Munich.

Bournemouth midfielder Alex Scott is also not selected after picking up an injury against Croatia in October.

The 23-year-old, who made his England debut in the first international break of the season and was described as "the complete package" by Tuchel, is reportedly in contention to return for the Cherries against Ipswich on Sunday.

Manchester United's Marcus Rashford and Newcastle's Tino Livramento, who both pulled out the last squad prior to any matches being played, also miss out with respective hip and knee injuries.

Como's Trevoh Chalobah is another absentee, while Manchester City's Nico O'Reilly and Rico Lewis have also not been selected.

James Garner of Everton and Nottingham Forest's Morgan Gibbs-White retain their places after being added to England's last squad prior to any fixtures being played, while Arsenal's Ben White and Inter Milan's Djed Spence return for the first time since the summer.

The Arsenal pair of Declan Rice and Ezri Konsa, who both withdrew from the last England party, are fit and selected.

An anti-aging drug may help protect the brain before Alzheimer's symptoms begin

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A drug commonly used to prevent organ transplant rejection may offer an unexpected new approach to protecting people at increased risk of Alzheimer's disease. Researchers at the University of Missouri have discovered that rapamycin, a medication also studied for its effects on aging, can improve blood flow in the brains of middle-aged adults who carry a gene variant linked to Alzheimer's.

The findings could help scientists develop strategies to address Alzheimer's risk long before memory problems appear. The research also adds to the University of Missouri's growing contributions to understanding and potentially preventing the most common form of dementia.

The study was led by Ai-Ling Lin, a professor in the university's School of Medicine and an investigator at the Roy Blunt NextGen Precision Health building. Her work focuses on identifying changes in the brain that occur before Alzheimer's symptoms develop and finding ways to intervene early.

Why Brain Blood Flow Matters for Alzheimer's Risk

A major focus of Lin's research is the APOE4 gene variant, which is associated with a substantially increased risk of developing Alzheimer's disease later in life.

People who carry APOE4 can experience reduced blood flow in certain areas of the brain years before they begin having noticeable memory difficulties. Because these circulation changes can occur so early, researchers believe they could provide an important warning sign of increased Alzheimer's risk.

Lin's latest findings suggest that improving brain circulation with an existing medication may be one way to address these early changes.

"Alzheimer's tends to happen more in older people, especially for those with APOE4," Lin said. "If we can slow down aging in the brain for those people most at risk, maybe we can reduce the risk of them developing Alzheimer's disease."

Rapamycin is already used in medicine to prevent the immune system from rejecting transplanted organs and to treat certain rare conditions. However, researchers have also been investigating its potential to slow biological aging and protect the brain.

Before joining the University of Missouri, Lin conducted experiments involving mice that carried APOE4. Her earlier research showed that rapamycin could slow brain aging and increase blood flow in these animals.

Those results prompted an important question: Could rapamycin produce similar benefits in people who have a genetic predisposition to Alzheimer's but have not yet developed the disease?

To investigate, Lin studied healthy adults between the ages of 45 and 65, including people who carried APOE4 and others who did not.

All participants received a low daily dose of rapamycin for four weeks. When researchers examined the results, they found a notable difference between the two groups.

Brain blood flow improved among participants carrying APOE4, but the same benefit was not observed in those without the gene variant. This suggests that rapamycin may have particular potential for people whose genetics place them at greater risk of Alzheimer's.

Although the study did not establish whether the treatment could prevent Alzheimer's or preserve memory over time, the improved circulation offers a promising direction for future research.

Women With the Alzheimer's Risk Gene Saw the Greatest Benefits

Another intriguing finding emerged when the researchers examined differences between male and female participants.

Women carrying APOE4 experienced the largest improvements in brain blood flow following treatment. That result may be especially important because women account for nearly two-thirds of people living with Alzheimer's disease.

"We also found that females with APOE4 saw the greatest improvement in brain blood flow, which is significant given that nearly two-thirds of people with Alzheimer's are women," she said. "This research is an example of precision medicine, as we work to identify who can benefit from this drug the most."

The results highlight the potential of precision medicine, an approach that considers individual characteristics, including genetics, when determining which treatments may be most effective.

Rather than assuming that a medication will benefit everyone equally, researchers hope to identify the people most likely to respond.

Moving Alzheimer's Discoveries Toward Human Treatments

Lin, who is also a professor in the university's College of Arts and Science, credits Mizzou's collaborative research environment with helping advance discoveries like these.

At the Roy Blunt NextGen Precision Health building, scientists and medical professionals work together in the same facility. This collaboration is designed to help move promising laboratory findings into human clinical studies more quickly.

The latest results provide an encouraging starting point for investigating whether rapamycin could eventually help delay or reduce Alzheimer's risk in genetically vulnerable individuals. More research will be needed to determine whether the changes in brain blood flow lead to lasting improvements in brain health or a lower likelihood of developing the disease.

The study, "EXPRESS: Rapamycin increases cerebral blood flow and modulates metabolic, inflammatory, and microbiome profiles in healthy middle-aged APOE4 carriers: a pilot single-arm trial" was published in Journal of Cerebral Blood Flow & Metabolism.

Scientists just found dinosaur blood that survived 115 million years

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Scientists have discovered remarkably preserved traces of dinosaur blood inside 115-million-year-old bones, revealing that delicate biological molecules can survive far longer than previously believed. The breakthrough could transform how researchers study prehistoric animals and even influence the search for ancient life beyond Earth.

An international team led by Curtin University found fossilized red blood cells and chemical evidence of blood molecules in the remains of Aratasaurus museunacionali, a meat-eating theropod dinosaur discovered in northeastern Brazil. The findings, published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, also include amino acids associated with collagen, an important structural protein found in bones and other connective tissues.

The discovery provides the first definitive chemical confirmation of molecules derived from hemoglobin inside fossilized dinosaur red blood cells. Earlier research had identified structures resembling blood cells in ancient fossils, but scientists had not conclusively demonstrated the presence of these particular molecular remains within them.

Ancient Dinosaur Blood Preserved Inside Fossilized Bones

When researchers examined the dinosaur's limb bones, they identified numerous mineralized red blood cells inside vascular canals, the tiny passages that once contained blood vessels. Chemical analysis revealed preserved heme molecules, which are components of hemoglobin, the protein that allows red blood cells to transport oxygen.

Finding these molecules provided direct chemical evidence that the fossil contained remnants of ancient blood. It also demonstrated that some biological materials once thought too fragile to survive for millions of years can remain detectable under exceptional conditions.

The study was led by John Curtin Distinguished Professor Kliti Grice, founding director of Curtin University's WA-Organic and Isotope Geochemistry Centre and an Australian Research Council Laureate Fellow.

"To detect intact heme-derived molecules in 115-million-year-old red blood cells is astonishing: this really is a landmark discovery," Professor Grice said.

"It not only extends the known fossil record of blood, but also offers a direct molecular window into the physiology of long-extinct animals so we may better understand them and the world they lived in."

How Dinosaur Blood Survived 115 Million Years

The extraordinary preservation appears to be connected to a mineral called hausmannite. This mineral helped stabilize the fossilized blood cells and preserve their molecular components (including amino acids of collagen) over an immense span of geological time.

In effect, the mineral created a natural time capsule that protected microscopic evidence of the dinosaur's biology long after its death.

Researchers believe the conditions surrounding the dinosaur's burial were also crucial. They suspect the animal was quickly covered by sediment in an environment with little oxygen and abundant microbial activity. These conditions may have helped preserve biological material that would ordinarily have broken down and disappeared.

The international research effort brought together scientists from Curtin University's School of Earth and Planetary Sciences, the Japan Agency for Marine-Earth Science and Technology, the Brazilian National Museum, the Regional University of Cariri, and Johns Hopkins University.

A Discovery That Challenges Fossil Preservation Limits

The findings challenge a longstanding assumption in paleontology that molecules associated with blood, particularly those derived from hemoglobin, could not remain preserved across such enormous periods of time.

Beyond confirming the survival of ancient blood components, the discovery raises the possibility that other fossils may contain molecular information that conventional examinations have overlooked.

"These findings challenge longstanding assumptions that certain biomolecules such as hemoglobin cannot survive deep time," Professor Grice said.

"It opens exciting new avenues for molecular-level paleontology, allowing us to investigate the evolution of blood, metabolism and even activity levels in ancient animals."

Such information could eventually help researchers better understand how extinct animals functioned, including aspects of their metabolism and physiology that are difficult to reconstruct from bones alone.

Implications for Ancient Life Beyond Earth

The significance of the discovery may extend well beyond dinosaurs. Understanding how fragile biological structures can survive for millions of years could also help scientists identify possible traces of ancient life in other extreme environments, including those beyond Earth.

Professor Martin Van Kranendonk, head of Curtin University's School of Earth and Planetary Sciences, said the results are changing how researchers understand the preservation of molecular structures over vast timescales and under harsh conditions.

These insights could be valuable to astrobiology, a field that investigates the possibility of life elsewhere in the universe. If biological molecules or their chemical signatures can persist under the right conditions for more than 100 million years, scientists may need to reconsider where and how they look for evidence of ancient organisms.

"This study exemplifies how coordinated efforts across institutions, continents and scientific disciplines can unlock extraordinary insights into ancient life," Professor Van Kranendonk said.

The research was supported by a 2021 Australian Research Council Laureate Fellowship awarded to Professor Grice.

The study, titled "Heme signatures in mineralized red blood cells within vascular canals of a 115- million-year-old dinosaur bone," was published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

Materials provided by Curtin University. Original written by Sam Jeremic. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

NASA’s Swift telescope falls to Earth after nearly 22 years in space

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

NASA's Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory has returned to Earth after spending nearly 22 years in space, bringing an extraordinary era of astronomical discovery to a close. The spacecraft re-entered Earth's atmosphere on Wednesday, ending a mission that lasted more than ten times longer than originally planned.

Launched in 2004, Swift was a pioneering space observatory equipped with three telescopes designed to study some of the most powerful explosions in the universe. Its instruments could detect different types of light, allowing scientists to investigate energetic cosmic events that would be difficult to understand using a single telescope.

NASA's Swift Observatory Re-Enters Earth's Atmosphere

The U.S. Space Force confirmed that Swift entered the atmosphere at 7:39 a.m. EST on Wednesday above North Sumatra, Indonesia. The reported location was approximately 3.4 degrees north latitude and 98.6 degrees east longitude.

NASA anticipated that most of the spacecraft would burn up as it plunged through the atmosphere. However, officials acknowledged that some components might have survived the intense heat of re-entry.

A Two-Year NASA Mission That Lasted Nearly 22 Years

Swift was originally developed to investigate gamma-ray bursts, extraordinarily powerful flashes of high-energy radiation associated with some of the most extreme events in the cosmos. These bursts can occur when massive stars collapse or when certain compact objects, such as neutron stars, merge.

Although NASA initially planned for Swift to operate for just two years, the observatory continued making scientific contributions for more than two decades.

One of its most important capabilities was its ability to respond quickly to sudden cosmic events. When Swift detected an energetic outburst, it could rapidly redirect its telescopes toward the source and notify observatories around the world. This coordinated approach allowed astronomers to study brief astronomical phenomena before they faded from view.

Swift also observed multiple wavelengths of light, giving researchers different ways to examine the same event and better understand the physical processes behind it.

Over its remarkably long lifetime, Swift's scientific contributions expanded far beyond its original focus on gamma-ray bursts.

The observatory helped astronomers investigate active galaxies powered by supermassive black holes, stars that produce powerful X-ray flares, and supernovae, the enormous explosions that mark the deaths of certain stars.

Its observations also extended much closer to home, including studies of comets and asteroids within our own solar system.

By combining rapid observations with the ability to detect different forms of radiation, Swift became a valuable tool for exploring both distant cosmic explosions and nearby objects. Its re-entry marks the end of a mission that greatly exceeded its planned lifespan and helped transform scientists' understanding of some of the universe's most energetic phenomena.

Materials provided by NASA. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Don't miss: Meteor showers, fireballs, and a supermoon light up November's night sky

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

November 2026 is packed with celestial events worth watching, from brilliant meteors streaking across the sky to a striking encounter between Mars and Jupiter. NASA's Voyager 1 spacecraft is also approaching an extraordinary milestone, while a dazzling supermoon will close out the month.

Three meteor showers offer multiple opportunities to catch shooting stars, including occasional fireballs bright enough to stand out dramatically against the night sky. Meanwhile, two of the solar system's most recognizable planets will appear unusually close together just before sunrise.

As if that weren't enough, humanity's most distant spacecraft is reaching a point where radio signals from Earth take an entire day to arrive.

Here's what NASA recommends watching for in November.

Three Meteor Showers Could Light Up November's Sky

November gives skywatchers several chances to see meteors flash across the darkness. The first opportunities come from the Southern and Northern Taurids, two related meteor showers that reach their highest activity levels about a week apart.

The Southern Taurids peak around November 5, followed by the Northern Taurids around November 12. Both showers get their names from the constellation Taurus, where their meteors appear to originate when viewed from Earth.

Neither shower is especially prolific. Even under excellent conditions, each typically produces only about five meteors per hour. However, the Taurids are known for occasionally producing impressive fireballs, exceptionally bright meteors that can make even a relatively quiet night of skywatching memorable.

The Leonids provide another opportunity later in the month. Their best viewing periods are expected during the early morning hours of November 17 and 18, particularly after the Moon has disappeared below the horizon. Under ideal conditions, observers could see approximately 15 meteors per hour, although actual counts will generally be lower.

For the best chance of spotting meteors, travel somewhere away from bright city lights. Allow your eyes roughly 30 minutes to become accustomed to the darkness, then watch a broad section of the sky rather than concentrating on one small area.

Mars and Jupiter Appear Almost Side by Side

Meteor showers aren't the only reason to get outside before dawn. On November 15 and 16, Mars and Jupiter will create an eye-catching pairing in the southeastern sky.

The two planets will appear separated by only about 1.2 degrees, slightly more than the apparent width of two full Moons placed next to each other.

Jupiter will be the brighter of the two, making it relatively easy to identify. Nearby, Mars will stand out with its characteristic reddish appearance.

No telescope or binoculars will be necessary to enjoy the sight. Both planets should be readily visible to the unaided eye under suitable viewing conditions.

Although they will appear remarkably close from Earth's perspective, Mars and Jupiter remain separated by enormous distances in space. Their apparent proximity is simply a result of their positions along our line of sight.

Watching the planets over several consecutive mornings will reveal another interesting feature of their motion. They will gradually appear to move closer together before their positions shift and they begin separating again.

Voyager 1 Reaches an Extraordinary Deep Space Milestone

While November offers plenty to observe in Earth's skies, something equally remarkable is happening far beyond the planets.

NASA's Voyager 1 spacecraft is reaching a historic distance of one light-day from Earth, becoming the first human-made object ever to travel that far from our planet.

A light-day is the distance light travels through space in 24 hours. Because radio signals move at the speed of light, a message transmitted from Earth will require an entire day to reach Voyager 1 at that distance.

If the spacecraft sends a response immediately, the return signal will take another 24 hours to arrive. That means even the fastest possible round-trip communication will require approximately 48 hours.

The milestone illustrates just how far Voyager 1 has traveled since leaving Earth and how challenging it has become to communicate with a spacecraft exploring such distant regions of space.

To get a sense of the spacecraft's current direction, look toward the constellation Ophiuchus, also known as the Serpent Bearer. Voyager 1 is traveling through space in that general direction, far beyond anything visible to the unaided eye.

NASA is marking the achievement on November 17 with its Shine Bright for Voyager's Light-Day activation, inviting people to celebrate this extraordinary accomplishment in space exploration.

November's Supermoon Begins a Three-Month Celestial Show

The final major skywatching highlight arrives on November 24, when a full supermoon will rise into the evening sky.

A supermoon occurs when a full Moon coincides with a period when the Moon is relatively close to Earth in its orbit. Because the Moon follows a slightly elliptical path rather than a perfect circle, its distance from our planet changes throughout the month.

When a full Moon occurs near the closer portion of that orbit, it can appear somewhat larger and brighter than an average full Moon. Although the difference is real, it is often subtle enough that casual observers may not immediately notice it.

November's event is particularly noteworthy because it begins a sequence of three consecutive supermoons. Another will follow in December, with a third arriving in January.

For an especially memorable view, NASA recommends looking toward the eastern horizon around sunset on November 24, when the full Moon will be rising.

The Moon may seem unusually enormous when it first appears near the horizon. However, much of that dramatic impression comes from a familiar visual phenomenon called the Moon illusion.

This optical effect makes the Moon appear larger when it is close to the horizon than when it is high overhead, even though its actual apparent size changes very little. The illusion can occur during any full Moon, not just a supermoon.

With three meteor showers, a close planetary pairing, a historic Voyager 1 milestone, and the beginning of a supermoon series, November offers plenty of reasons to look up and explore the night sky.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Dispatch director Chris Rebbert has shared his thoughts on the much-requested Malevola romance, and it’s not looking good for a potential second season.

When Dispatch first released a little over a year ago, it very quickly garnered a big community that was eager to see what each new episode brought. I’d argue that things really blew up in the fourth episode when Robert was forced to choose between going on a date with Blonde Blazer or hanging out with Invisgal.

That tough decision turned Dispatch from a hit into a phenomenon, with romance being all that most fans could talk about. It wasn’t just about Mandy and Courtney, though, as the Dispatch community quickly started begging for more options, with Malevola easily being the number one pick. AdHoc has poked fun at the requests before, but it’s now very clear just how realistic the romance is.

Dispatch stars Laura Bailey, Travis Willingham, and Alanah Pearce on the superhero adventure game from former Telltale talent.

Rebbert goes on to say that he doesn’t think that “Malevola has any romantic or sexual interest in Robert” and that she “just enjoys toying with him and making him uncomfortable”, in reference to the scene in Episode 6 that most took as a sign. Malevola is apparently “too cool” for anyone to have a relationship with.

The director even points to the scene as an important lesson for the player that “not everybody wants to be with you”. I don’t think that lesson was well learned.

Considering Rebbert is literally the director of Dispatch, it’s fair to say what he ‘believes’ is true is very likely the case. While AdHoc could give in to fans and try to force a relationship into the still unconfirmed second season, it’s pretty obvious from that comment and all of the previous jokes that it’s not really a possibility. Iranians face looming war threat with no reliable info

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Most people in Iran have little idea of the course of the US-led war and peace talks on the global stage. With few sources of information, the population must navigate conflicting rumors and fears of a nuclear attack.

"There is a constant sense of fear in the air," a 37-year-old mother in Tehran told DW. "Every time I hear a loud noise, I flinch. Even when a door slams, I jump — thinking that bombs are falling."

Now, unlike in the opening months of the war, her children are back at school.

"I don't work, but keeping the children at home out of fear is not a solution," she said. "They would go stir-crazy in our small apartment."

Schools remain open across Iran despite the military standoff with the US and Israel. However, some parents whose children attend private schools have received messages from those schools explaining what to do in the event of a renewed war or other emergency.

Numerous parents have shared such messages on social media.

Irans Education Ministry has tried to reassure the public.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"People should not pay attention to rumors spread with the aim of sowing fear and forcing schools to close," Ali Farhadi, a ministry spokesperson, said in a recent media interview.

Farhadi said the ministry would decide whether the schools stay open or close, and would do so in coordination with the security authorities. Should the security outlook change, the situation will be reassessed accordingly, the official said.

Parallel to Farhadi's appeal to disregard rumors aimed at raising fear, state media themselves are discussing scenarios of a possible nuclear attack.

On November 2, Iranian state television broadcast an advisory on protective measures in case of a nuclear strike. People were advised to remain in a shelter for at least 24 hours in the event of such an emergency.

Many in Iran fear that the United States is preparing to use nuclear weapons against the "Pickaxe Mountain" underground bunker about 220 kilometers (130 miles) south of Tehran. US President Donald Trump has hinted at attacking the location, which is close to Iran's Natanz nuclear facility, but made no explicit mention of using nuclear weapons.

Iran's government denies that any work related to its nuclear program is being carried out at the mountain bunker.

The fighting between the United States and Iran has mostly petered out in recent months as the Trump administration focused on the midterm election. But two days after the election, on November 5, the US news outlet Axios reported that the US military was making final preparations for a possible resumption of large-scale strikes against Iran.

At the same time, there are also hopes that a Democrat-controlled Congress would force Trump to scale down his military ambitions.

"It is still difficult to make any concrete assessment of the implications of the US midterm election results for the war with Iran," Hamidreza Azizi, the International Crisis Group's consulting senior Iran analyst, wrote in response to questions from DW.

Iran's government sees the election results as a victory, Azizi wrote. The regime's resistance to the Trump administration has contributed to the Republican Party's loss in the US midterms, and some in Tehran are already seeking to frame the result as a sort of revenge for the US-Israeli assassination of senior Iranian leaders in the opening stages of the war.

Against this backdrop, Iran is likely to take an even harder line on its core demands.

Trump has at least two months before the new Congress takes office, Azizi wrote. He could decide to resume strikes on Iran in an attempt to offset a domestic political setback by targeting a foreign policy victory.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Qatari mediators are trying to persuade the Iranian and US governments to agree on a draft peace deal. The aim is to prevent another round of escalation and pave the way for the resumption of direct talks in the coming days.

But even well-connected journalists in Iran have little information on the substance of the talks, which are closed to the public.

In a post on X on October 31, the Iranian journalist Hadi Mohammadi wrote: "Domestic media outlets are completely in the dark about negotiations that directly affect people's everyday lives. They have to wait for reports from Axios, Al Arabiya, Al Jazeera and other international media outlets. In such an environment, developing our own narratives and doing journalism becomes pointless."

As the fog of war persists, many in Iran no longer have the energy to keep up with the media reporting. A 72-year-old retired woman from western Iran told DW she now deliberately avoids the news.

"I hardly have the strength to think about it anymore," she said. "I'm just glad that my three children have emigrated."

Global temperatures are set to exceed 1.5 degrees above pre-industrial levels in 2026, likely the second-warmest year on record

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Climate scientists say the world is set to breach the critical 1.5-degree Celsius threshold in 2026. This year is also on track to become the second-warmest on record.

Global temperatures will almost certainly average more than 1.5 degrees Celsius (2.7 degrees Fahrenheit) above pre-industrial levels in 2026, the European Union's Copernicus Climate Change Service said Friday.

That would make 2026 the second calendar year after 2024 above the threshold set as part of the Paris Agreement.

The Paris treaty was aimed at preventing global temperatures from rising above 1.5 degrees Celsius in the long term. The 2015 deal was seen as a major step forward in tackling climate change.

Most of the world's nations agreed to hold global temperature rise to well below 2 degrees Celsius compared to pre-industrial levels and "pursue efforts" to keep it below 1.5 degrees.

The target concerns long-term warming, so a single year above 1.5 degrees does not in itself mean it has been breached.

Copernicus estimates that 2026 will most likely be the second-warmest year after 2024 since records began. However, there is a 20% chance that it could surpass 2024 levels.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The climate service also expects the four-year average for 2023 to 2026 to be above the 1.5 degree threshold.

October 2026 was the warmest October on record globally, with an average global surface temperature of 1.75 degrees above pre-industrial levels for the month. It marked the third consecutive month above the 1.5 degrees threshold.

"An exceptionally strong El Nino is adding further heat to a climate already warmed by human activities, while 2026 is on track to become the second calendar year above 1.5 degrees," said Samantha Burgess of the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts.

The UN Environment Programme now expects warming to overshoot the Paris Agreement's 1.5-degree target.

Because this threatens drastic climate extremes and dangerous tipping points, the overshoot should be kept as small and short as possible, it said.

According to a September report by the UN Environment Programme (UNEP), every additional fraction of a degree makes the risks and impacts worse.

Climate scientist Johan Rockström, director of the Potsdam Institute for Climate Impact Research, called for temperatures to be lowered quickly again by removing CO2 from the atmosphere.

UNEP lays out a scenario in which the world sees warming peak at 1.7 to 1.8 degrees Celsius around mid-century, before falling back to 1.5 degrees Celsius by 2100. Getting there would require steep emissions cuts of 69% by 2040, relative to 2025 levels.

It was now unavoidable that global warming would exceed 1.5 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels within the next five years, Rockström said at a briefing.

He said that humanity was currently on "a path to an unmanageable future."

I watched a humanoid robot clean my apartment. It felt like a taste of the future.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The startup Tau Robotics is sending humanoid robots to clean San Francisco homes. They still need lots of human guidance, but they’re learning.

I’m not the sort of person who typically pays for a house cleaner. I’m too frugal, and I kind of enjoy putting on a podcast and going to town on my tub with a Magic Eraser. But when I saw an X post about a new humanoid-robot cleaning service in San Francisco, I immediately signed up for the waitlist. I wanted my apartment to feel like The Jetsons, even if just for an hour.

I was also motivated by journalistic interest. In order to ensure an authentic experience, I didn’t tell Tau Robotics, the startup behind the X post, that I was a journalist—though I did use my real name, so it’s possible someone looked me up. Tau’s long-term goal is to make autonomous cleaning robots a reality, but they’re not quite there yet: For now, the robots are piloted remotely by humans, who watch what’s going on via cameras mounted in the robots’ heads and wrists. As the robots clean, they collect data that Tau will use to train up their more independent descendants. Every counter successfully wiped and T-shirt successfully folded will help the models that power the robots learn to do better on the next go-round.

Because this data is so valuable to Tau, the company charges less for cleaning than one might expect for such a cutting-edge product. At the time I booked, Tau was offering $30 for an hourlong cleaning with a single robot; now the charge is $60 per weekly cleaning with two robots, or $100 for a one-time visit. But even at the cheaper rate of $30 per hour, Tau’s robots aren’t a great bargain. They are thorough but slow, so if you, like me, are tempted to stand around watching them for their entire visit, it would probably be a better use of your time to just take care of the cleaning yourself.

But the robots are physically capable of cleaning, and they’re undeniably cute. Tau has armed each four-and-a-half-foot-tall robot with a 3D-printed “head” with antennae that allow it to communicate with Tau’s headquarters, as well as two 3D-printed claw “hands” (the more humanlike alternatives apparently aren’t durable enough, and claws do surprisingly well for most cleaning tasks). Mine was wearing a Tau Robotics shirt, and grippy socks so that it didn’t scratch hardwood floors.

With all the human support they need, the robots are closer to radio-controlled cars than Waymos. Many tasks, such as making the bed and folding laundry, require what’s called kinematic guidance: The human pilot performs the necessary motions, and sensors transmit those movements to the robot, which copies them. These kinematically guided tasks produce especially valuable training data. Tau’s AI models might not have solved laundry folding yet, but learning from the examples of robots that have completed the task in the real world is how they’ll get there.

But according to Tau cofounder Cornelia Weinzierl, to whom I came clean as a journalist a few weeks after the cleaning, the robot can already complete some other tasks—such as vacuuming and mopping—without any human help. If Tau succeeds in using all the data it is collecting to train truly autonomous cleaning robots, they could even become San Francisco’s newest tech tourist attraction as Waymos become more commonplace countrywide.

The cleaning robot that came to my building last month was already a bit of a spectacle. It arrived in a blue minivan, out of which it was helped by Weinzierl and another Tau employee. That’s when the robot encountered its first obstacle: the eight stairs leading up to the building’s front door. I’d come down to greet Weinzierl and the robot, so I had a front-row seat to its struggles with my steps. It climbed as if it were drunk. Each successful step was as likely as not to be followed by a stumbling backward stagger that erased all its progress—though, impressively, it never entirely lost its footing.

When I later discussed my experience with Weinzierl, she explained that the robot sometimes walks strangely because it is operated remotely from Tau’s headquarters, and the short signal transmission delay can affect its coordination. “The amount of work and time that we spend just optimizing, for example, timings and connectivity is crazy,” she said.

Inside, the carpeted staircase leading up to my apartment defeated the robot entirely, and Weinzierl had to lug it up the last few steps. This, it turned out, was not typical. As the robot cleaned, it repeatedly stalled out, something Weinzierl attributed to it overheating in the unseasonably hot weather—until she heard from one of her colleagues that the heat had shut down not the robot but the equipment that its human controller was using to watch its progress through my apartment. Without remote assistance, the robot couldn’t clean, and so it, and Weinzierl, departed before my booked hour was up. (Back in September, Weinzierl or another Tau representative remained with the robot throughout its visit, but she says that’s no longer necessary.)

You can’t fault Tau on customer service, though. Weinzierl quickly emailed me to apologize, comp the visit, and offer me another on the house (minus whatever price I assign to my data privacy)—and that was all before I told her I was a journalist. I scheduled the next cleaning for about a month later, after a couple of trips out of town, and it went much more smoothly—the robot got up my stairs without much assistance, though still with the drunken stagger.

In the hour it was there, it did a creditable job of cleaning my kitchen (though it didn’t brave the stove) and even saved me my most dreaded cleaning task, the toilet. That was its most impressive performance: Three times the head fell off its toilet cleaning wand, and three times it managed to reattach it by poking around with the handle until it made contact at just the right angle.

The robot is still rough around the edges, which is to be expected for a product in beta. It repeatedly left my taps dripping, it knocked over a (closed) bottle of olive oil, and it couldn’t manage to right an air purifier that it had toppled. But I easily forgave all those missteps because of how charming it was. As I watched it wipe down my countertops, I felt the same fond gratitude that I feel when a Waymo delivers me safely home after a night out.

Still, it’s not all slapstick and whimsy. If Tau Robotics or its competitors succeeds in training robots to clean autonomously and inexpensively, people could lose their livelihoods, and the United States is a long way away from implementing policies to take care of those left unemployed by AI.

But Weinzierl maintains that someday turning over cleaning work to robots could make a lot of sense. “Cleaning is not a job that a lot of people really actively want to be doing—there’s high turnover, there’s a high risk of injury,” she said when we spoke after the cleanings. “We need people, and we don’t have them, and no one wants to do it.” She said that the company is also starting to consider someday deploying the robots for other dangerous or physically challenging jobs, such as mold remediation and home health care.

Tau Robotics still has a long way to go: The amount of data needed to train robots to do the varied and complicated tasks of apartment cleaning is probably enormous, and the company has completed only a couple hundred cleanings so far. But it’s remarkable that there are now humanoid robots able to clean your toilets and mop your floors, even if they need quite a bit of human help to do so. The Jetsons no longer feels quite so far away.

Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible.
