What to know about the IIHF Women's Ice Hockey World Championship

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As ice hockey's governing body continues to wrangle over how to respond to a CAS order to reinstate Russia to international play, the top women's tournament gets underway in Denmark without Russia.

Since it's inception in 1990, the Women's Ice Hockey World Championship has almost always been held in the spring – apart from 2021 and 2022, when the COVID-19 pandemic saw it moved to the summer. This year, though, the tournament, being hosted by Denmark from November 6 to November 16, is being held in November for the first time. The idea is make it easier for all of the world's best players to participate. Since its inception in 2023, the North America-based Professional Women's Hockey League (PWHL), widely considered the world's best, has had to pause its season to allow its players to take part in the Worlds. This is no longer necessary, as the PWHL's regular season doesn't open until December.

"This gives the women's game a unique selling point," International Ice Hockey Federation (IIHF) President Franz Reindl recently told the SID news agency. "Because the PWHL season begins after this, we can be certain that the very best players are participating."

Previously, the teams were divided into a stronger group and a weaker group, with the idea being to avoid, as much as possible, lopsided scores. This is because women's hockey is not as balanced as the men's game, with Canada or the United States having won every tournament since its inception. Under the old system, all five teams in the stronger Group A advanced to the quarterfinals. Under the new format, four of the five teams advance from both groups. This also sees the two powerhouses, the USA and Canada in separate groups, meaning the marquee matchup can't occur early in the tournament.

Group A: USA, Czechia, Switzerland, Germany, Austria

Group B: Canada, Finland, Sweden, Japan, Denmark

No. While the Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) ruled on October 30, 2026 — a week before the opening faceoff of the Women's Ice Hockey World Championship — that the IIHF must allow Russia back into its tournaments during the 2026-2027 season, it made no mention of this tournament. Under the CAS ruling though, the IIHF will be required to include Russia at the 2027 tournament in Quebec City.

As noted above, either Canada or the United States has won every edition of this tournament – and indeed the two countries have split Olympic gold medals between them (Canada 5, USA 3) since the sport was introduced to the Winter Games in 1998. There is nothing to suggest that this is about to change in Herning and Esbjerg, although Canada enters the tournament with a vastly different roster from the one that won Olympic silver in Milano in February. Among those looking to break this North American domination are Finland, the only other team ever to have made it to a World Championship final and Switzerland, who took bronze in Milano.

The United States and Canada are having to play in generic kits provided by the International Ice Hockey Federation's official supplier due to an ongoing commercial rights dispute. The Canadian federation, Hockey Canada, said in a statement on Wednesday that its players would be wearing jerseys designed by True Hockey instead of their Bauer jerseys featuring the national team logo. USA Hockey has confirmed that its players too will be wearing jerseys without its national emblem.

"While we have yet to see the jersey Canada will be playing in on Friday, our team remains focused on the tournament and representing our country proudly on and off the ice,” Hockey Canada said.

Zambia's civil service overhaul raises questions

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Government-backed retirements and institutional realignments are fueling debate in Zambia, with opposition politicians questioning transparency while officials defend the changes as necessary reforms.

Many people living in Zambia seek careers in the civil service because of the stability and security traditionally associated with government employment. But recent retirements, dismissals and restructuring exercises across several public institutions have unsettled that perception and sparked a national debate about the government's use of "national interest" provisions.

Among those affected is former police public relations officer Godfrey Chilabi, whose departure from the Zambia Police Service was announced in October. Choosing not to publicly discuss the circumstances surrounding his exit, Chilabi simply told local media that he had been retired in the national interest.

"Yes, I have been retired in national interest. I can't give any reason, but know I'm in retirement."

Chilabi's departure came amid a series of changes affecting public institutions in Zambia.

Charity Munganga, a Zambia Police Service deputy inspector general, was dismissed, and all staff members at the Zambia Medicines and Medical Supplies Agency were let go as part of a "reset" following a government audit.

Additionally, all directors in senior management at the Zambia Environmental Management Agency (ZEMA) were put on administrative leave to facilitate an institutional realignment exercise.

The affected ZEMA directors will be assessed for their competence, performance and suitability for their positions before a final decision is made on whether they will be reinstated or separated from the institution, according to an October 20 statement by the Ministry of Green Economy and Environment cited by news portal Mwebantu.

The issue moved to the center of public debate in October when opposition lawmakers questioned the government in Parliament over retirements and dismissals taking place across public institutions following Zambia's August 2026 general election.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Governance and political commentator Andrew Nguvu told DW that retirements carried out in the national interest raise important questions about transparency, fairness and the protection of a professional public service.

"If at all, there are legitimate reasons for retiring any particular civil servant, then obviously the government should clearly explain [these reasons]," Nguvu told DW.

He argued that public institutions should operate according to established procedures and the law rather than political considerations that could undermine institutional independence.

Zambian law allows service commissions to retire established officers in the national interest under provisions governing public service employment. Previous governments have also used the measure. However, the current exercise has generated significant scrutiny because it is occurring alongside broader restructuring efforts affecting multiple institutions.

Critics argue that the issue is particularly sensitive because Zambia's ruling United Party for National Development (UPND) previously opposed the use of retirements in the national interest while in opposition.

As a result, opposition lawmakers, led by Stephen Kampyongo, have criticized the measures, which they view as inconsistent with the ruling party's earlier position.

Kampyongo, speaking during Vice President's Question Time in the National Assembly on October 17, asked what assurances the government was giving public servants "working in fear" and what measures were being taken to ensure those retired in the national interest did not become destitute.

Responding on behalf of the government, Vice President Mutale Nalumango rejected suggestions that the exercise amounted to a political purge and instead described it as part of a lawful process of institutional realignment.

She acknowledged concerns surrounding the changes while defending the government's authority to make personnel adjustments within the public service framework.

"As provided for in section 39 of the Public Service Pensions Act, there are situations that arise and people fall by the way side. [It is] unfortunate that a life was lost, but maybe it is an issue that public servants/public service workers must brace themselves for," she told Parliament.

Nalumango's comments have been cited by critics as evidence that additional changes could occur as the government continues restructuring public institutions.

The debate has also been amplified by comments from UPND National Youth Chairperson Gilbert Liswaniso, who argued that governments naturally seek to work with people aligned with their vision.

"Every political party [that] comes to power, has to flush out! [It] has to work with the people aligned with the vision of that political party!"

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

For critics, such statements reinforce concerns that political considerations may be influencing decisions within the public service.

Civil rights activist David Chishimba rejects the notion that perceived political affiliation should determine a person's future in government employment.

"The Constitution, however, is very clear on this. It restricts public officers from participating in partisan politics while in office. This safeguards an impartial civil service. However, it does not prohibit private political belief or otherwise," he told DW.

Chishimba added that civil servants serve the country rather than any political organization. "Civil servants serve Zambia, not a party, the president or a campaign."

For public workers caught in the middle of these administrative realignments, the shift is deeply unsettling, noted Nguvu, the political commentator.

The measures come amid high living costs and household debt burdens in Zambia.

According to Nguvu, abrupt personnel changes can affect morale throughout the public service and leave workers uncertain about their professional future.

"This can weaken morale and help to destroy institutional memory. Most of these decisions lack clear explanations," he said.

Nguvu argued that concerns become more acute when explanations for personnel decisions are not communicated clearly.

The reported dismissals have also raised concerns among civil society groups about due process and accountability.

Chishimba, who is also president of the Zambia Council of Activists (ZACA), called for greater transparency from the authorities.

"ZACA respectfully calls on the Government to publish the names and evidence of those facing dismissal, submit every case to due process, and allow the Civil Service Commission to assert its constitutional independence."

Czech arms makers set sights on space defense in bid to sustain Ukraine-driven arms boom

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Czech arms makers have thrived on demand linked to the war in Ukraine. But to sustain their growth, they need to look beyond ammunition and toward new technologies — and even space.

Czech arms maker Czechoslovak Group (CSG) announced in mid-October that it has acquired a stake in Canadian space technology manufacturer MDA Space.

This space mission is part of the company's bid to continue riding the boom that has transformed its fortunes in recent years.

When it listed on the Amsterdam Stock Exchange earlier this year, CSG soared to become the most valuable company in Central and Eastern Europe, with a market capitalization of €14.6 billion ($16.4 billion), propelling its owner, Michal Strnad, to the top of the region's rich list.

CSG began life as a 1990s startup, selling military surplus amid the collapse of the Warsaw Pact, the military alliance between the Soviet Union and Europe's socialist Eastern Bloc nations, which was founded in 1955 and disbanded in 1991.

It has since grown to become the second-largest producer of ammunition in Europe after Germany's Rheinmetall and now produces across Europe and in the US.

The main driver of the company's rising fortunes has been Russia's war in Ukraine, which has driven a dramatic boom across the Czech defense industry as a whole.

In 2021, the sector's exports totaled €600 million. In 2025, these exports had expanded to €4.7 billion, powered by orders placed by Kyiv and its European allies.

By the end of that year, European states had procured approximately €11 billion worth of military support for Ukraine from Czech defense firms.

Orders to the value of roughly €4 billion have arrived via the Czech Ammunition Initiative, a program that coordinates the transfer of artillery shells to Ukraine.

Amid the avalanche of orders sparked by Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, CSG and Czech peers such as STV, Colt CZ and PBS have been busy expanding production to pump out military vehicles and planes, guns, grenades, shells and bullets.

"The war in Ukraine, particularly in its early stages, was especially well suited to Czech firms," Krzysytof Debiec of the Centre for Eastern Studies (OSW) told DW.

Debiec says that this was largely due to the major role that the former Czechoslovakia played in supplying Warsaw Pact countries.

"Specialization in the production of ammunition — including Soviet calibers — the repair of Soviet-era equipment and the manufacture of relatively unsophisticated systems that could be rapidly introduced and were compatible with the Ukrainian army's existing stocks," have all been key, he explained.

At the same time, European states eyeing the threat from the east and broader geopolitical instability are boosting their own defense budgets.

"The shift in US policies vis-a-vis NATO is forcing European nations to increase their defense spending dramatically, and that's another factor" in the Czech defense industry boom, Jan Jires, a former deputy defense minister and now a senior advisor at the International Institute for Strategic Studies, told DW.

However, since launching on the stock exchange, CSG's share price has performed erratically, illustrating the shifting sands on which Czechia's booming defense industry now stands.

The government in Prague has responded to the sector's surge by transferring the defense industry portfolio from the Ministry of Defense to the Ministry of Industry and Trade to align its growing role in exports with national economic and trade policy.

At the same time, the populist ruling coalition that took power a year ago is made up of parties that have either promised to lower support for Ukraine or — in the case of the far-right Freedom and Direct Democracy party (SPD) — are openly pro-Russian.

As a result, the coalition has halted contributions to the Czech Ammunition Initiative and is dragging its feet on raising defense spending to meet NATO targets.

This only limits an already small domestic market, leaving the industry dependent on exports for about 90% of its revenue — the vast bulk of which is still linked to Ukraine — and lacking a stable base for expansion.

"The Czech industry has outgrown the country's financial means," said Jires. "Unlike, German, French or British companies, Czech manufacturers cannot rely on domestic demand to make them globally relevant."

Increased global defense spending looks set to continue, but an end to Russia's war in Ukraine, a change in US foreign policy or a calmer geopolitical environment would hit Czech arms manufacturers hard. For this reason, they must ponder what the future of war, and its funding, might look like.

The conflict in Ukraine offers some indicators, having become increasingly focused on swarms of drones and a technological race between Kyiv and Moscow to improve the performance of the unmanned aircraft.

Major questions must now be asked regarding "the size and role of the military, how it is equipped, what should be purchased and when new weaponry can arrive" Andor Sandor, a defense analyst and former commander of Czechia's Military Intelligence Service, told DW.

For a company like CSG, for which bullets and shells currently account for around 60% of sales, these are vital questions.

"Czech companies — many of which still focus on relatively unsophisticated military production — must invest more in innovative manufacturing," said Debiec, who notes that most are well aware of the challenge. "They have reinvested their extraordinary profits and are already more technologically advanced and diversified in their production than they were in 2022."

CSG's acquisition of MDA Space is part of such efforts.

The company, which already held interests in British and German space technology companies, pointed to the close link between the space and defense industries when it announced the deal.

Noting that global government spending on space defense accelerated to €65.83 billion last year, CSG estimates that the overall space economy will triple in size to €1.6 trillion over the next decade.

Developing capabilities in space is "a natural extension" of its activities, the company stated, noting plans for further development in the segment, including a "dedicated space team."

To power such missions, Czech defense companies must make sure they're in a position to tap into funds for defense and space under the next EU budget, which could rise as high as €130 billion, said Jires.

"If they fail to do so, and all this money is grabbed by German, French and Italian companies," he warned, "Czech manufacturers may miss the opportunity to truly scale-up."

Russian extremist group denies sending hoax bomb threats

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Passengers on this Aer Lingus flight to Rome were diverted to a military base in France after a bomb threat

The "Male State", an extremist misogynistic group named in this week's hoax bomb threats to Italian airports, has denied any link to the false warnings causing flight disruption across Europe.

The comments to the BBC came as reports of more bomb scares to airlines began appearing, including on a Ryanair flight from Rome to Lisbon and a Wizz Air flight from Prague to Rome which was diverted to Venice.

Bomb threats were also reported to a number of high-profile Irish media organisations on Friday, prompting evacuations and a statement from the Irish government suggesting a "co-ordinated campaign of disruption".

Replying to the BBC Russian Service, an account on Telegram - one of several linked to the so-called "Male State" and its founder Vladislav Pozdnyakov - mocked any suggestion the group was involved in emailing the threats.

"What Male State? Where have you seen it? It hasn't existed for many years," the message on the channel reads. "If tomorrow they sign a [threat] letter, say, from Putin, will you then try to reach our president to ask your stupid question?"

The message claimed that "fighters" of the "Male State" had planted explosives on two incoming flights

The group was banned in Russia in 2021 and Pozdnyakov is now thought to live abroad. It has since renamed itself in some places as "Male Legion".

The initial hoax email, seen by the BBC, was sent on Thursday at 07:21 with the subject title: warning of a threat.

It was addressed to several offices and individuals at Aeroporti di Roma (ADR), the airport managing company in Rome, as well as accounts at the city's Fiumicino airport itself. The originating email address seems to be linked to the state health services in Bogota, Colombia.

It's not clear whether other countries' airports or airlines received the same or similar threat. At least one warning made on Friday appears to have come by phone, not email.

Written in word perfect Italian, the bizarre text sent to Rome on Thursday claimed that "fighters" of the "Male State" had planted explosives on two incoming flights in an attack on Italy's "feminist regime" that would be "remembered forever".

One flight was diverted as a result - ordered to land in France â another was escorted into Rome by Italian air force jets, scrambled for the second time this week. No explosives were found on either plane, or any other that reported receiving bomb threats.

Asked why this particular email was taken so seriously, a source at ADR in Rome told the BBC that such warnings were extremely unusual. They didn't remember anything similar in recent years.

Despite the Russian group's denial, Italy's La Stampa newspaper appears in no doubt that "Male State" was behind the threats, asking in an article whether it is acting as "the regime's armed wing".

"Attacking, and being able to deny the attacks...is part of the Russian game plan," the paper writes, external.

One Telegram channel linked to Pozdnyakov is mainly filled with virulently pro-Russian messaging about the war on Ukraine and other posts highlighting problems and tensions in Europe, including recent youth protests.

The online investigation team at Bellingcat, who studied the group back in 2022, called it then a "vocal and vile online booster" of the Russian invasion, with a clear extremist far-right ideology.

As the Europe-wide hunt continues for who is behind the spate of hoax mails, and whether they are being co-ordinated - or could be the work of copycats - Italy's defence minister has warned of a "strategy to increase chaos".

Guido Crosetto noted that he had been talking of the "hybrid threat" to Europe for some years.

"It seems to me that, day after day, from incidents involving refrigerators to the blood supply in France and now these false alarms, there are those who have an interest in fuelling tensions," the minister said on Thursday, referring to a spate of recent cyber attacks that boosted supermarket fridge temperatures, destroying mountains of produce.

"Our interest is to do the opposite and ensure the country's security," Crosetto said.

Kenyan doomsday cult leader given life sentence for murdering 191 people

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mackenzie was found guilty in October of the murder of 190 children and one adult

A Kenyan court has sentenced the leader of a doomsday cult, Paul Mackenzie, to 200 years in prison for murdering 191 people, all but one of them children.

Alongside 28 others, Mackenzie was found guilty of the murders for instructing members of his cult to starve their children to death so they could "meet Jesus".

Justice Diana Mochache said Mackenzie bore the "primary criminal culpability" for ordering his followers to fast to death, in one of the world's deadliest episodes of cult activity.

"The sentence is appropriate and just, to send an unmistakable message that such atrocities warrant the maximum penalties allowed by law," the judge added.

The self-styled pastor was arrested in 2023 after the discovery of more than 400 bodies in shallow graves in Shakahola forest near the Kenyan coast. The sprawling investigation into the cases shocked Kenya and drew international attention.

The court sentenced Mackenzie to 100 years in prison for homicide and another 100 years for offences related to starvation.

The sentences within each category will run concurrently, but the two sets will run consecutively, bringing his total prison term to 200 years.

Mackenzie also faces separate manslaughter and terrorism charges, which he denies.

Prosecutors had sought the death penalty, arguing that the scale and circumstances of the killings warranted the harshest punishment.

However, Justice Mochache declined to impose the death penalty, despite ruling that the case met the threshold for its consideration.

"Nevertheless, the court declines the invitation to impose the death penalty on any of the victims," she said, adding that capital punishment would eliminate any prospect of rehabilitation and disengagement. The death sentence has not been carried out in Kenya since 1987.

Mackenzie was tried alongside 28 accomplices, including guards who enforced his fasting orders and gravediggers who helped conceal the victims' bodies in mass graves.

They received varying prison sentences of up to 160 years.

Justice Mochache described the killings as "cold and calculated", saying the perpetrators had shown no remorse and had even declared their willingness to fast to death themselves.

Before the hearing began on Friday morning, Francis Wanje, who lost eight relatives including two grandchildren in Shakahola forest, told the BBC's Newsday programme that he was "hoping for... a sentence which will match his [Mackenzie's] deeds in the forest".

Mackenzie and the co-accused had asked for leniency from the court.

Before their conviction on 19 October, they had all pleaded not guilty and repeatedly insisted that nobody was forced to die.

More than 400 bodies were dug up in Shakahola forest

Mackenzie's followers believed he was "Jesus's mouthpiece", Justice Mochache said as she delivered her eight-hour judgement last month.

Those who opposed Mackenzie's teachings were beaten to death.

One of his co-accused said he had told his followers that anyone who "resisted the directive to fast must be forced to go to heaven through kicks and blows".

Burials inside the forest where the cult was based were called "weddings" and those who died "were celebrated as having wedded with Jesus" the judge said.

The judge found that the deaths arose from an organised system, including religious teachings and directives that compelled followers to follow the deadly fasting.

Post-mortem results showed the cause of deaths to be starvation for most victims, while others died from suffocation, strangulation or head injuries.

The cause of death could not be determined for some, with their bodies having decomposed, while others died soon after their rescue as they were so emaciated.

The Shakahola deaths have shone a spotlight on the lack of oversight of rogue religious activities and how they could have carried on for so long.

The gravity of the case prompted President William Ruto to set up a commission of inquiry, although this was later declared unconstitutional.

Investigations by the state-funded Kenya National Commission on Human Rights (KNCHR) revealed that local administration and law enforcement officials had repeatedly turned a blind eye to the cult's activities.

"We expected at that time the police would take action immediately to go and rescue the other members there, but it didn't happen," said Wanje, who reported the case to the police in March 2023.

Wanje said a relative of his in the police force and a former cult participant had managed to save his grandson at the time. The child told the police what had happened and showed them the grave of his siblings.

"It was [supposed to be] his last day.. they [would have killed] my grandson."

The cult leader had previously been arrested and later released for lack of evidence.

Mackenzie established Good News International Ministries in 2003.

Over the next 16 years, the church grew in popularity, drawing thousands of followers in its branches across Kenya.

The church ran a television station called Times TV which would broadcast his sermons from his church in Malindi.

His End Days sermons included conspiracy theories that described education as "evil", healthcare as "against the doctrine" and digital identity cards as "the mark of the devil".

The main church shut down in 2019 and Mackenzie moved to Shakahola forest, encouraging his devotees to follow him.

While the conclusion of the murder cases may bring a sense of justice for the victims and their families, the convictions only account for 191 victims, while more than 400 bodies were exhumed from the forest.

Two dead after bus crashes in France, officials say

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

French gendarmes and rescue workers at the scene of the crash in Lent, eastern France

One child and one young adult have died after a bus crashed in eastern France, local authorities have said.

The bus was travelling between Lyon and Bourg-en-Bresse when it crashed at around 07:00 local time on Friday in the town of Lent, around 34 miles (55km) from the border with Switzerland.

The town's mayor Yves Cristin told French media the bus "left the road and hit a tree in the ditch".

The bus was carrying 43 passengers, including 15 children, the Bourg-en-Bresse prosecutor's office said. Three people are in a critical condition and a further 13 have sustained minor injuries.

A young girl and an 18-year-old man have been identified as the two who died in the incident by authorities. However, there have been differing reports as to the age of the girl.

The prosecutor's office said it was "opening an investigation into the incident, on charges of road homicide and aggravated road traffic injuries".

The driver has been taken to hospital after sustaining injuries and has not yet been questioned by police, the prosecutor's office added.

Photos circulating in French media show a blue and white bus tilted in what appears to be a ditch on the roadside. Trees line the side of the road. The front of the bus appears to be heavily damaged.

Local mayor Cristin told BFM TV: "I arrived minutes after the accident and people were in shock.

"The side of the bus was torn off, and clearly, the people who were on that side must have been seriously injured."

Addressing weather conditions at the time of the crash, the mayor told Le Figaro "there was a bit of fog, the road was wet" but there were "no major difficulties".

Speaking to the same paper, Fabrice Pannekoucke, head of the Auvergne-RhÃ´ne-Alpes region, said: "At this stage, there are no particular explanations or interpretations that can be given."

French Transport Minister Philippe Tabarot said the bus did not operate "exclusively on school routes" but that there were "many schoolchildren on board".

Earlier, French Education Minister Ãdouard Geffray said it was "always difficult when our children are victims of accidents of this nature".

Emergency services were at the scene and medical helicopters were deployed to take victims with serious injuries to a specialised hospital.

More than 100 firefighters and 50 gendarmes were also at the scene.

The local town hall was opened to provide shelter and coordinate with emergency services, the local mayor said.

$3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

$3.1B in Stolen Binance Crypto Has Yet to Be Laundered: Elliptic

Around half of the $6.43 billion stolen from Binance last month has yet to be laundered, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in a report updated on Friday.

About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly in ETH and Bitcoin, and those funds are "very likely" still held by North Korea, Elliptic said. A further $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, mostly stablecoins that issuers such as Tether and Circle froze within an hour of the theft. That compares with around 3% frozen after the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, according to Elliptic.

Binance hack update: the XRP has started moving. About $50 million has been swapped, mostly through THORChain into BTC, with about $226 million still to move.

Bitcoin converted from other stolen assets is now about 1,650 BTC ($124 million), almost twice the figure a day earlier.…

The FBI has attributed the October 29 theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor. North Korea has denied involvement.

The laundering so far has been "highly complex and clearly automated," Elliptic said. It has used more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions, roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds.

The 18,270 BTC stolen from Binance, worth about $1.5 billion at the time of the theft, was split into 19 addresses on the day of the hack and has not moved since. The nine largest Ethereum addresses, holding 89% of the attacker's ETH, have not moved since October 30.

Most of the laundering so far, about $220 million in TRX, has run through Tron, where a single distribution wallet sent 684.6 million TRX to hundreds of new addresses over 51 hours, including through the night in Pyongyang. Almost all of it was bridged to Ethereum.

All of the stolen Solana, about $87 million at current prices, has been swapped, bridged or mixed, and the attacker has started laundering the stolen XRP, with about $50 million converted so far and $226 million remaining. In total, about $124 million has been converted into Bitcoin, much of it through cross-chain service THORChain.

Some funds have been mixed. About 33 BTC, worth around $2.5 million, has gone through Wasabi-style coinjoins, and about $1.1 million in ETH and SOL has been deposited into the Privacy Cash pool, which paused deposits for about a day.

The speed has fueled speculation that AI agents are moving the funds, but Elliptic said the evidence points to conventional automation. "The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," the firm said.

Elliptic expects the attacker to swap the ETH for Bitcoin and then cash out through OTC brokers and exchanges over a period of months. After the Bybit hack, North Korea laundered all 499,000 stolen ETH within 10 days but took months to cash out, the firm noted.

Binance has continued to restore services. On Friday, the exchange said users could now verify that their balances are backed at least 1:1 using the zero-knowledge proof for its latest proof-of-reserves snapshot, and that it aims to reopen withdrawals for all tokens on restored networks by the end of next week.

Tesla hikes Cybertruck prices up to 10% after announcing it will kill production

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Tesla has raised Cybertruck prices in the US by up to 10% and stopped taking orders for the base Dual Motor AWD trim. The cheapest Cybertruck you can actually order now costs $92,490.

The move comes right after Tesla confirmed it will end Cybertruck production at Gigafactory Texas around mid-2027.

Here’s what Tesla’s online configurator shows today, compared to the prices set in August:

The Dual Motor trim still shows a new, higher price on Tesla’s website, but the option is greyed out. So Tesla raised the price of a version it isn’t selling anymore.

This is the second price hike in less than three months. Tesla raised prices by $5,000 on two trims in August, when the Dual Motor went from $69,990 to $74,990 and the Premium AWD from $79,990 to $84,990.

Add it up and the Premium AWD is now $12,500 (15.6%) more expensive than it was in early August. And the entry price for any Cybertruck you can order went from $69,990 to $92,490. That’s a 32% jump in about 10 weeks.

For reference, Tesla unveiled the Cybertruck in 2019 with a promised starting price of $39,900.

The price hikes make little sense from a demand standpoint. Tesla doesn’t break out Cybertruck deliveries, but its “Other Models” category, which also includes the Model S, Model X, and Semi, fell 48% year-over-year to 8,295 units in Q3. We estimate Cybertrucks made up 7,000 to 8,000 of those, about 12% of the line’s 250,000-unit annual capacity.

Tesla also tried the opposite approach earlier this year. It offered a dual-motor Cybertruck at $59,990 in February for “only the next 10 days,” then pulled it. A single-motor RWD version was discontinued after just 173 sales. None of it moved the needle.

The Cybertruck isn’t sold in Europe or most right-hand-drive markets, and Tesla canceled it for Australia and New Zealand last month.

This is what a wind-down looks like. Tesla isn’t trying to sell more Cybertrucks anymore. It’s trying to make as much money as possible on the last ones, with the hope that announcing the end of production will encourage some people to secure their order.

Once you’ve decided to end a program, volume stops mattering. You have a set number of build slots left, a parts supply you want to burn down, and probably a small group of fans who’ll want one before it’s gone. Pausing the cheapest trim and charging $7,500 more for the rest is the obvious play. You push everyone into higher-margin configurations and stop pretending the base truck is a real product.

But it does say a lot about the last two years of Cybertruck pricing. Tesla went from a $59,990 “limited-time” offer in February to a $92,490 entry price in November. That’s a company that never figured out who the truck was for.

Altra Makes a Non-Zero-Drop Shoe? Altra Experience Wild 3+ Review

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Altra has been one of the most popular footwear brands on America’s long trails for the past decade. They’re known for two things: foot-shaped designs and zero-drop shoes. In 2024, Altra broke the mold by releasing the Experience Wild, its first non-zero-drop trail runner. The shoe kept Altra’s signature foot-shaped design but added a 4 mm heel-to-toe drop.

Altra updated the original model in 2025 with the Experience Wild 2, then introduced several upgrades in the Experience Wild 3 series in 2026. The Experience Wild 3+ pairs a new midsole with a tried-and-true Vibram outsole for an elevated trail experience in a low-drop shoe.

I’ve worn Altras exclusively since my first thru-hike. On the AT in 2021, I switched between the Lone Peak and the Olympus. On the PCT in 2025, I wore several Olympus models, including the Olympus 275. It’s safe to say I have plenty of experience hiking in Altra’s zero-drop shoes.

I was excited and a little nervous to test a low-drop shoe after getting used to zero-drop. Over the summer, I put more than 300 miles on my pair of Experience Wild 3+ shoes. I took them on multiple backpacking trips in Great Smoky Mountains National Park and logged plenty of trail-running miles in East Tennessee and Western North Carolina. I even ran my first 50K trail race in them, so they saw their fair share of 20+ mile days on the trail during training.

It helps to understand a few basic footwear terms before diving into the technical specs.

Outsole: The bottom of the shoe that touches the ground. Different brands use proprietary rubber compounds to help the shoe grip the terrain. This is where the traction comes from. The outsole often includes lugs, or the chunky elements on the bottom of a shoe that dig into the ground and improve grip and stability.

Midsole: The cushioning between your foot and the outsole. This is usually made from foam, and brands often use their own proprietary blends. Cushioning generally falls into three categories: low, mid, and high.

Upper: The top part of the shoe that holds your foot in place and protects it. Uppers are often made from mesh designed to promote breathability while helping keep water and debris out and preventing your foot from sliding around.

Heel-to-Toe Drop: The height difference between the heel and forefoot. This measurement can range from 0 mm to 8 mm or more.

Stack Height: The amount of material between your foot and the ground. Basically, it determines how high off the ground your foot sits inside the shoe. A higher stack height means more material underfoot, while a lower stack height brings you closer to the ground.

In recent years, trail runners have become the new standard for many hikers. According to The Trek’s 2025 Appalachian Trail Thru-Hikers Survey, 87% of respondents wore trail runners for the majority of their hike. Altra lists the Experience Wild 3+ as suitable for both trail running and hiking. The features that make trail runners appealing to runners are just as important to hikers: adequate cushioning for long days on the trail, aggressive traction, and a breathable upper.

Altra’s Experience Family includes the Experience Flow for roads and the Experience Wild for trails. Within the Experience Wild line, there are three models, with the main differences being the outsole and upper.

The Experience Wild 3 is the base model, featuring a MaxTrac outsole, Altra’s proprietary rubber compound. The Experience Wild 3+ has the same features as the base model but upgrades to a Vibram Megagrip outsole. Finally, the Experience Wild 3+ GTX swaps the standard mesh upper for a waterproof GORE-TEX Invisible Fit upper. All three models use the same Altra EGO P35 foam midsole.

There isn’t anything new or flashy about the Experience Wild 3+ upper. It’s Altra’s standard mesh, which I found to be breathable and quick-drying. Altra advertises a redesigned heel collar, and I found the padding around the collar provided enough support to keep my foot secure.

The Experience Wild 3+ uses Altra EGO P35, a proprietary foam blend designed to feel lightweight and responsive. Altra categorizes its cushioning into three levels: Low, Mid, and High. The Experience Wild 3+ falls into the Mid category.

Although different foam materials can affect how a shoe feels, Altra’s cushioning categories are based on stack height. The shoe’s stack height is 32 mm at the heel and 28 mm at the forefoot, giving it a 4 mm heel-to-toe drop.

The shoe also has a rocker shape — a slight curvature that helps position your foot for toe-off as you move into your next step.

One of the biggest upgrades from the base-model Experience Wild 3 to the 3+ is the Vibram Megagrip outsole. Vibram is an industry leader in engineering durable rubber compounds, and Megagrip is designed to provide reliable traction on challenging terrain. The addition of Traction Lugs and an integrated toe bumper is intended to improve grip and protection, helping you scramble confidently across wet or dry surfaces.

Altra is known for its FootShape design, which features a wider toe box that allows your toes to splay naturally instead of feeling cramped in a narrow, pointed shoe. Altra offers three FootShape Fits: Original, Standard, and Racing. The Experience Wild 3+ falls into the Standard category, which Altra describes as providing room for your toes while maintaining a secure fit through the midfoot.

Many hikers wear gaiters to keep small rocks, twigs, and other debris out of their shoes. The integrated GaiterTrap makes attaching a gaiter easy right out of the box. There’s no need to superglue Velcro to the back of your shoe or fuss with how to attach the gaiter to your laces.

I was concerned about noticing a difference when transitioning from a true zero-drop shoe to a low-drop model. 4 mm doesn’t seem like much, but I wasn’t sure how my calves and Achilles tendons would feel.

Honestly, I barely noticed the difference, aside from the sensation that the shoe was gently propelling me forward. It had been a while since I’d worn a shoe with a rocker, but the slightly elevated heel made me feel poised for my next step, ready to push off with my toes. I’m happy to report that I didn’t experience any unusual or uncomfortable tendon pain. That’s something more common when switching to zero-drop shoes rather than going in the opposite direction.

I really appreciated how lightweight these shoes felt. At 3.5 oz lighter than the Olympus 7, the difference was noticeable, especially when trail running. The Experience Wild 3+ weighs about the same as the Lone Peak, and I felt lighter on my feet compared to the bulky Olympus. Sometimes, wearing the Olympus feels like I’m running around in clown shoes! That wasn’t at all how these shoes felt.

The new Altra EGO P35 foam felt bouncy and provided ample cushioning without feeling cumbersome. The modest stack height offered plenty of support, but I never felt like I was wearing high heels or had lost my connection to the ground beneath my feet.

Hiking in the southern Appalachians almost guarantees wet and muddy conditions, with plenty of opportunities to get your feet wet during water crossings. In the Experience Wild 3+, I confidently bounded across slick rocks and wet roots. The Vibram outsole did its job well, gripping whatever terrain I encountered.

The large traction lugs also added stability and support, and I liked how aggressive the traction felt on the trail. For an extra $15 over the base model, I think the Vibram Megagrip outsole is worth the upgrade from MaxTrac.

Two thoughtful features make these shoes ready for the trail straight out of the box: the built-in GaiterTrap and appropriately sized shoelaces.

It may seem silly, but I’ve found the laces on some other Altra models to be too short. When I wear my shoes a little looser instead of cinching them down tightly, I barely have enough lace to double-knot them. That wasn’t a problem with the Experience Wild 3+.

I also love the GaiterTrap. I don’t have to fuss with the attachment kits that come with many gaiters, and I can get out on the trail without making any modifications to my shoes.

Most thru-hikers are no strangers to 20+ mile days. In the Experience Wild 3+, my feet started feeling fatigued after about 15 miles. I chalk this up to the moderate cushioning, especially since I’m used to the high cushioning of the Olympus. I didn’t initially notice rocks and roots underfoot while hiking and running, but my feet started feeling tender as I pushed towards the 20-mile mark. I wore them for my 50K on the Bartram Trail, and my feet were hurting by the finish.

I think this could be a common complaint with shoes that have low-to-moderate cushioning, including the Experience Wild and Lone Peak lines. It’s worth noting — especially for thru-hikers, who spend 12+ hours a day on their feet.

During the months I wore the Experience Wild 3+, I didn’t have any problems with blisters. It wasn’t until my ultramarathon that I developed a gnarly heel blister. I suspect this had more to do with the pace and demanding terrain of the course than the shoes themselves, but it’s worth mentioning.

I do think these shoes fit true to size, so I wouldn’t necessarily recommend sizing up from the start. However, if you’re prone to blisters, especially around your heels, it may be worth considering a half size up. This is particularly important if you plan to spend a lot of time hiking on steep trails.

I was honestly really impressed with these shoes! After putting hundreds of miles on them, I didn’t find many glaring flaws or features that got on my nerves. They were mostly comfortable, durable, and worked well for me. The pros certainly outweighed the cons, making the Experience Wild 3+ a solid choice for hikers.

I expect this shoe will continue gaining popularity, especially among hikers who haven’t worn zero-drop shoes before. When I transitioned from boots to trail runners, I worried I’d experience the growing pains that can come with switching footwear. The Experience Wild 3+ could be a great gateway to low-drop and zero-drop shoes, and I think Altra is onto something with this design.

Shop the Women’s Altra Experience Wild 3+

Anna is from western North Carolina and grew up romping through hollers and hills. Her love of the Appalachian mountains drew her onto the Appalachian Trail in 2021 where she became "Magic". The AT was her first thru hike and the place where she would meet her future husband, Truffles. After completing the AT, the pair moved to east Tennessee to work as backpacking guides in Great Smoky Mountains National Park. Dead: Tesla Cybertruck Production to End Next Year

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

CEO Elon Musk posted on his X account that Tesla will soon end production of its battery-electric pickup truck.

The Tesla Cybertruck will soon meet its maker, per a post by Tesla CEO Elon Musk on his personal X account. Though it was first shown as a prototype in 2019, the Cybertruck finally materialized in production form for the 2024 model year.

According to Musk’s post, “production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year,” indicating that the 2027 model year will be its last. Admittedly, it’s possible production moves elsewhere. This seems unlikely, though, given the middling sales figures of the model, which continue to fall far short of the annual 250,000-unit production figure predicted early on by Musk.

Of course, the battery-powered pickup’s high price didn’t help its cause. Currently, Tesla’s limiting Cybertruck orders to the north of $90,000 Premium All-Wheel Drive and the six-figure Cyberbeast trims. The more than $80,000 Dual-Motor All-Wheel Drive variant remains listed on the site, indicating Tesla may open orders for that model again before production ends.

Ramayana's Connection To Christopher Nolan's Epic Explained By Director & Star

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Ramayana star Ranbir Kapoor and director Nitesh Tiwari have addressed the epic's similarities to Christopher Nolan's adaptation of The Odyssey.

Directed by Tiwari, Ramayana is a two-part adaptation of the ancient Indian epic, starring Kapoor as Rama, Sai Pallavi as Sita, and Yash as Ravana. The story follows Rama, a prince whose journey involves love, exile, and a battle against powerful forces. Meanwhile, Christopher Nolan's The Odyssey, starring Matt Damon as Odysseus, adapts Homer's Greek epic about a warrior's journey home following the Trojan War. Despite their different origins, both epics feature a significant scene involving a bow.

In an interview with ScreenRant's Liam Crowley, Kapoor and Tiwari discussed the parallels between Ramayana and The Odyssey, particularly their respective bow scenes. When asked whether he noticed the similarities or intentionally leaned into them, the director explained that he believes Homer's story may have drawn inspiration from the Indian epic.

"Ramayana is the oldest epic, and I believe Homer was inspired by Ramayana in some way; having written those bits in Odyssey. And yes, I did notice those equivalents and I felt very happy that our greatest epic has got a tribute in another epic which belongs to the West side, and it's going to make it very easier for the people to also connect with what we were trying to bring to the West also."

Kapoor also recognized the similarities between the two stories, but emphasized that filming Rama's bow scene was particularly meaningful.

"To be honest, it's written in our text 4,000 years ago. So actually, when I saw Odyssey, I thought, 'That's from Ramayana.' Exactly. But yeah, sweet coincidence. I love The Odyssey. I love Christopher Nolan. But yeah, doing Ramayana had a very different feeling."

Identify the cover art while scratching off as little foil as possible.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

The veteran Bollywood actor described the significance of the scene, in which Sita's father holds a ceremony to determine who is worthy of marrying his daughter. The challenge involves lifting a powerful bow, with Rama ultimately taking part in the moment.

"So it was just goosebumps when we were performing that scene, when we were doing the whole lifting of the bow, even though it was not that heavy and I was acting like it was, but I could feel it when I was lifting it above my head. I just had some weird current going up and down just to be part of such an iconic moment."

The comparison is especially interesting given that both epics use a bow to highlight their central heroes. In Homer's Odyssey, Odysseus strings his bow and shoots an arrow through twelve axe heads, helping him reclaim his home from Penelope's suitors. Meanwhile, in Ramayana, Rama lifts and breaks Shiva's bow to win Sita's hand in marriage, marking an important moment in Rama's larger journey.

Although Tiwari believes the similarities could suggest a connection between the ancient texts, there is no established historical consensus that Homer directly drew inspiration from Ramayana. Nevertheless, the filmmakers believe the parallels could help Western audiences connect with the Indian story. While Christopher Nolan's adaptation makes several changes to Homer's original epic, the bow scene remains a notable connection between the two stories.

Ramayana: Part One is currently playing in theaters, with a second installment set to continue the epic story.

Ramayana is the first part of an epic duology that brings the ancient Indian epic to the big screen. This film adaptation explores the legendary tale of Prince Rama, his quest to rescue his wife Sita, and the ensuing battle against the demon king Ravana.

Christopher Nolan's The Odyssey is a 2026 adaptation of Homer's epic poem, chronicling Odysseus's treacherous journey home from the Trojan War.

10 Can’t-Miss New Fantasy Series and Duologies Releasing First Installments Before the End of the Year

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This year has treated fantasy enthusiasts to new installments in some of the most popular series from Dungeon Crawler Carl to ACOTAR and beyond. And we're not done yet, as 2026 still has a few highly anticipated sequels on the way, including a brand-new Brandon Sanderson Cosmere novel.

But for those eager to get in on the ground floor of an exciting new series, November and December have plenty to offer in the way of new beginnings, too. Many beloved fantasy authors, including Cassandra Clare and John Gwynne, are kicking off new series before the year is out. For those looking for a new series to fall in love with, that's great news.

Starting a new series as soon as book one releases does have drawbacks, mainly waiting forever for the next installment to come out. Even so, there's something exciting about being among the first to discover a new fantasy hit before it really takes off. If that's your kind of reading experience, you won't want to miss some of the most promising new fantasy series that are starting off in late 2026.

For Dungeon Crawler Carl fans eager to experience a fast-paced blend of sci-fi and fantasy that delivers on wit and action, Cello's Gate is a must-read new release that hit shelves on November 3. Cello's Gate, which centers on sky pirates, is Africh's traditional publishing debut. It's also the first title in the new Sky Pirates of Imperia series, which has a confirmed second book already on the way.

In Cello's Gate, readers will meet Captain Grey and his crew of sky pirates. Known for pulling off particularly difficult missions, the crew finds themselves tangled up in an even more difficult job than usual. They're tasked with tracking down the legendary Stones of Indigo. There's just one problem. They don't exist.

Romantasy fans no doubt recognize Danielle L. Jensen's name from her wildly popular A Fate Inked In Blood. Now that the second book in that duology is out, Jensen is ready to take readers on their next slow-burn, enemies-to-lovers fantasy adventure. Defy the Dusk, released on November 3, is the first installment in the new Sundering Duet duology.

In this introduction to the series, protagonist Lirien was the one who ripped the world apart. Now, creatures of the night roam the earth, with the most privileged survivors taking refuge on the Dawnrider. Lirien has spent 20 years planning to usurp the ship's tyrant king, but her plot is thwarted when she's kidnapped by the leader of those fighting back against the night. Now, she's questioning everything she thought she knew about the world and her place in it.

John Gwynne has written his fair share of beloved fantasy novels, most notably his Norse-inspired The Bloodsworn Saga series. Now, he's back with a brand-new series, one that leans even further into dark folklore and unsettling horror elements. Wolves of War, released on November 3, kicks off The Wolfheart Chronicles.

In this thrilling introduction to a new epic fantasy series, we find a land where the once-free Clans have been conquered by outside forces. Main character Ivan only wants to keep his daughter, Halia, safe, no matter who's in charge. But her strange powers, often viewed as a curse, could be the key to surviving what's to come.

Best known for her beloved Shadowhunter Chronicles, Cassandra Clare has become a staple in the YA fantasy space. Now, she's returning to the Shadowhunter universe one final time with a brand-new trilogy, The Wicked Powers. Released November 3, The Last King of Faerie is the first installment in Clare's latest Shadowhunter project.

The Last King of Faerie centers on Drusilla Blackthorn, a fourth-year student at Shadowhunter Academy. She finds herself held captive by Faerie prince Ash Morgenstern, who is as captivating as he is dangerous. But Drusilla is far from the only one in trouble. Kit Herondale and Ty Blackthorn become unwilling allies in the quest to save everything they hold dear.

You can purchase The Last King of Faerie here.

This LitRPG series started as a web serial, but is making its way to physical print for the first time this November. The first book in the series arrives November 10th, ushering in the print run for a beloved fantasy epic perfect for fans of Ready Player One and Dungeon Crawler Carl.

Vivienne was a top-tier gamer, hitting the max level in her VRMMO of choice, The Seven Cataclysms. But when she wakes up to find that she has become her mage, Vivisari, thrust into the world of the game, it isn't quite what she expected. It's been a hundred years since the game she knew came to its close, and the world has changed in that time. But even if the rules have changed on her, Vivienne must find a way to survive.

You can purchase New Life as a Max-Level Archmage here.

This Wretched Alchemy is a new debut romantasy from Tina Mars, and it's been getting a good bit of early buzz. Releasing November 10, this book kicks off the Ruinous Fate Saga, a new high-stakes dark fantasy steeped in slow-burn romance goodness.

In This Wretched Alchemy, readers step into a world fractured by noble houses at war. Divine fragments of power called god-shards offer a coveted chance to unlock new, extraordinary abilities to those who find them. Revel's brother died chasing a god-shard, and now, she's forced to take on the impossible task in his stead. Betrayal, a dangerously tempting enemy-turned-ally, and high-stakes action ensue.

You can purchase This Wretched Alchemy here.

Chances are, if you know Jesse Q. Sutanto, it's for her beloved cozy mystery series that started with Dial A for Aunties. But now, Sutanto is turning her literary talents to the fantasy genre for the first time with a new dark fantasy duology. An Edge Sharp Enough releases November 17, introducing readers to the beginning of a new two-part series.

In An Edge Sharp Enough, readers follow the story of an assassin, scholar, con man, and guard racing to save the world. Mara has no memory of life before she became an assassin, Livya's quiet life as a scientist is upended with an unexpected kidnapping attempt, and her childhood friend Kaian is her unlikely ally. And of course, Captain Fonde, a faithful city guard, finds himself mixed up in the whole mess, which just might bring the entire world as he knows it into question.

You can purchase An Edge Sharp Enough here.

Vampire fans, this one's for you. The bestselling author of The Spanish Love Deception returns with the first installment in a new paranormal romance series. Alma Vampires kicks off Armas' new The Icarus University series when it arrives on November 17.

This first book introduces us to Valle Cepeda, whose life changes in a flash when she becomes the sudden heir to the Alma fashion empire. But it's even more complicated than she realizes, as the mysterious Hadrian Cruz is here to collect on an ancient blood debt. He might be able to protect her, but she doesn't trust him, even if she's drawn to him in a way she can't explain.

Can we ever really have too many King Arthur retellings? Not as long as they offer a compelling new take on the legend, as far as many fantasy readers are concerned. And that's precisely what For the King, the first book in a new trilogy, aims to do. This debut novel from podcast host Carissa Weiser puts a new, time-twisting spin on the legend you think you know.

In For the King, the land of Logris lies waiting for a leader who can end years of constant political turmoil. But the mysterious Merlin has his own plans for fate, drawing Princess Morgan and young Arthur into his plans. As their futures begin to take shape, the Ladies of the Lake wait and watch.

The bestselling romantasy author behind the Nytefall Trilogy is back with the first book in a new series. The Bloodfire Oath is the first installment in The Arcanum Order, a new romantasy trilogy. This first book arrives on December 1, with book two already confirmed to be on the way.

In The Bloodfire Oath, magic is closely guarded by the Arcanum Order. Meanwhile, Scythe struggles to make ends meet as a paid assassin. But soon enough, she gets tangled up with the Guild, pulled into the Arcanum Order as an apprentice. Her new teacher could be her most important ally in this strange new world... if she can trust him.

Wall Street's fear gauge may be flashing a buy sign

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There's nothing like a good selloff to turn a trader into an investor – so the saying goes. It's an adage that echoes the sentiment of an even older one: buy when there's blood in the streets. The maxims ring true after Thursday's selloff in stocks that sent the Cboe Volatility Index (VIX) to its biggest one-day move since April 2025's "Liberation" week for tariffs, according to data from Bespoke Investment Group. In the 19 instances since 1990 in which the VIX rose at least five points from under 20 – it surged more than 11 points from 16.6 to 28.2 Thursday – the median return for the S&P 500 Index three months later was 4.2%, and 4.8% six months later, according to Bespoke analysis that also showed returns were positive 68% and 79% of the time over each period, respectively. "These types of sharp spikes from placid levels are like a line of thunderstorms coming through in summer," said Paul Hickey, co-founder of Bespoke. "The sky quickly darkens and wind gusts, but the loudest thunder and brightest lightning doesn't last long."

To be sure, the data do show the storms can be disruptive near-term. While the median return one month after such events was 2.1%, the average return was negative 0.4%, thanks to a few big downdrafts following volatility surges in 1990 and more recently, Feb. 24, 2020, when the Covid selloff was just getting started. According to X account OddStats, there were only 12 other days in stock market history prior to Thursday when the S&P 500 lost 4% and the Nasdaq 100 Index lost 6% – including one during Liberation week 2025 and three instances during the 2020 pandemic.Tech volatility was even more pronounced as the Nasdaq 100 posted its worst day since March 2020. The Cboe Nasdaq Volatility Index VXN jumped more than 13 points to 38.3, the highest since April 11, 2025. The selling was so intense in chipmakers that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) traded below its net asset value. Yet options traders were still leaning bearish in their trades into the bell Thursday, with 670,000 puts traded out of the total 900,000 options on the ETF. Of the total $1.7 billion options premium exchanged, $1.5 billion of it was in puts, according to SpotGamma.

An A320 Like No Other: Airbus Jet Completes Commissioning Flights With Eurofighter Nose

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

One of the world's rarest and most interesting examples of the Airbus A320-200 has just completed a series of commissioning flights wearing a new nose cone as part of a research program concerning the Eurofighter Typhoon. As Airbus explained in a statement that it released earlier today, the aircraft is being "used to test and further develop the Eurofighter Typhoon's s next-generation radar system."

Most recently, this work saw the jet, which is known as the Advanced Technology Research Aircraft and thus bears the rather apt registration D-ATRA, complete a series of five commissioning flights between October 7 and November 5. Sporting a customized pointy Eurofighter nose, the jet stands out compared to other A320s with their rounder noses, and it has been used as a testbed for 20 years.

Airbus confirmed on Friday, November 6, 2026, that D-ATRA had undergone final validation and completed its commissioning flights out of Ingolstadt Manching Airport (IGS), a German military base in the state of Bavaria. The multinational European planemaker is working with the German Aerospace Center (Deutsches Zentrum für Luft- und Raumfahrt / DLR), which has owned the aircraft for two decades.

Considerable structural modifications were required in order to support the Eurofighter nose, and D-ATRA actually flew with it attached for the first time back in early 2025. However, it had not yet been fitted with the new radar system. This has now been installed, and, after ground tests, it has been put through its paces in the air using the special A320 with the Eurofighter nose. Airbus's Sebastian Ritz explains:

"It is a distinguished platform that has already spent years flying as a research aircraft. We have taken it and repurposed it for our needs."

The bulk of 2026 has been relatively quiet on the flying front for D-ATRA. According to Flightradar24, it flew from Braunschweig Wolfsburg Airport (BWE) to Hamburg Airport (HAM) in February and then onwards from Hamburg to Ingolstadt in April, after which it sat parked for close to six months. Recently, however, it has been up in the air again, capturing the imagination of users of Simple Flying's flight tracker.

Between October 7 and November 5, the jet completed five circular commissioning flights that started and ended in Ingolstadt. The first of these on October 7 was also the longest, lasting three hours and 46 minutes. This was followed six days later on October 13 by one that lasted three hours and six minutes, while D-ATRA was also in the air for a further two hours and three minutes a week later on the 20th.

That was the shortest of its five test flights, with the fourth one on October 29 clocking in at two hours and 45 minutes. The commissioning program came to an end earlier this week on Thursday, November 5, 2026, when D-ATRA spent two hours and 48 minutes flying around the south of Germany. During this time, it reached a maximum altitude of 27,000 feet, as it cruised in a rectangular pattern east of Ulm.

The testbed for the latest and greatest radar under development for the Eurofighter Typhoon is an unusual looking model of the ubiquitous Airbus A320.

According to data from Planespotters.net, D-ATRA is 29.8 years old, having first been delivered to German carrier Aero Lloyd back in March of 1997. It stayed with the airline for more than seven years, eventually departing for Austrian airline Niki in May of 2004. During its time at Aero Lloyd, it also spent three lease periods away from the carrier at Ryan International Airlines in the United States of America.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

The jet had been registered as D-ALAE while at Aero Lloyd, and it initially retained this registration while at Niki. It then switched to the Austrian registration of OE-LOE, and spent just under two years flying under this designation before departing to join the DLR as the research testbed aircraft D-ATRA in June of 2006. It was re-registered as F-WWDB that December, but, in 2012, it reverted to D-ATRA.

New Miami art space to champion photography, film and video

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Mitrani Art building in Miami's Little River neighbourhood Photo: Javier Agustin Rojas

A beloved Miami art space returns this month, opening on 30 November in Little River as Mitrani Art, a photography, film and video venue with exhibition galleries, a screening room and artists’ studios. It is a long-awaited new chapter for the Mitrani Warehouse, which sisters Dina and Rhonda Mitrani began out of their family’s longtime Wynwood clothing factory in 2002, watching the neighbourhood around them transform into a thriving arts district.

“We want to continue that same model that we had in Wynwood, creating a community with art studios and event spaces,” Dina tells The Art Newspaper. “We still want to welcome emerging and established artists to use it as a place to experiment and create new work.”

The inaugural exhibition is a group show of lens-based art titled ReExposures: The Magic Within. One expected highlight is Suddenly We Jumped by the Miami-based artist Antonia Wright. The video captures a live performance from 2013 in which the artist actually shattered a glass ceiling, dramatically jumping onto a pane of glass and falling to the floor below.

“Antonia makes really creative work and focuses on important social issues that need change,” Rhonda says. “This one is very impactful.”

Both Mitrani sisters left their native Miami for university, and found their way to New York after graduating.

The co-founders of Mitrani Art, Rhonda Mitrani (left) and Dina Mitrani (right) Courtesy Mitrani Art

Dina worked at Christie’s and then at two different galleries, including one specialising in Latin American photography. After having children, she decided to move home and open a photography gallery in her home city. Rhonda, who had a thriving film production career, was more reluctant to return.

“I had no intention of leaving New York,” she admits. “I ended up making a documentary film about my father going back to Cuba, and that brought me back to Miami to finish editing it. During one of those visits, Dina said, ‘Why don’t you stay here? There’s something going on in the art community.’”

At Mitrani Warehouse, the sisters started small, with art studios on the second floor, and gradually took over the building. Dina, who has a photography background, started a contemporary photography gallery, Dina Mitrani Gallery, and Rhonda, a filmmaker, opened the Screening Room, the first dedicated space in Miami for filmmakers and video artists to show their work.

Tatiana Parcero, Actos de Fe #27, 2003 Courtesy the artist and Mitrani Art

But as the years passed, the sisters watched as artists and galleries slowly got priced out of the neighborhood. “We realised the Wynwood Art District was shifting. We held on for as long as we could, because we had an emotional attachment to that space where our parents had their business, our grandmother worked there, so many cousins, uncles,” Dina says. “But we realised it was time to go.”

When the Mitrani Warehouse closed in 2017, the sisters immediately began looking for a new location. In 2019, they purchased a mechanic shop in Little River with high ceilings and space to expand by building a separate studio facility. To design the new facility, they hired R&R Studios, a local multidisciplinary architecture firm run by Argentine artists Roberto Behar and Rosario Marquardt, who were longtime residents of the Mitrani Warehouse studios. The project took considerably longer than expected due to the pandemic, permitting and other roadblocks.

Marina Font, Untitled (celebration), 2014 Courtesy the artist and Mitrani Art

“It’s been seven years in the making, so we’re very excited to open. There’s a lot of anticipation,” Rhonda says.

The opening of Mitrani Art means a new permanent home for the Screening Room and Dina Mitrani Gallery, which have relied on pop-up programming across the city since the closing of the warehouse. The sisters have also started a new non-profit, Mitrani Art Projects Inc, that will run educational programmes in the space.

And artists are already moving into the new building, which has four studio spaces, each big enough for two artists. Rent is at the lower end of the market rate for the neighbourhood, the sisters say, and they hope to keep it that way by securing grant funding in the future.

Blue Origin unveils more thrust, less haste as New Glenn slips to 2027

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Leaked staff memo pushes return to flight into January, overshadowing third-stage and engine announcements

New Glenn's return to flight has slipped to January 2027, according to a leaked staff memo from Blue Origin CEO Dave Limp, overshadowing the company's announcements of a new third stage and booster engine.

Blue Origin has been rebuilding after its New Glenn rocket exploded during a ground test in May, extensively damaging the launchpad.

A month later, the company's CEO, Dave Limp, said that Blue Origin "still plan[s] to fly again this year," which raised a few eyebrows in the space and rocket community. The detonation had pretty much destroyed the facility, and getting back into the launch business so quickly seemed optimistic.

That timetable has now slipped. In the memo circulating on social media, Limp praised the team's efforts but said: "We're proactively updating our anticipated launch date to January 2027."

A small slip given the damage, but one that leaves less breathing room before NASA's planned Artemis III demonstration mission, involving lunar technologies from Blue Origin and SpaceX, next year.

The delay emerged as the Bezos-backed company unveiled GS3, a third stage for New Glenn, designed for missions including direct injection into geostationary orbit and Earth-escape trajectories. The company said: "GS3 is already under contract for a cislunar mission with a government customer scheduled to launch in 2028."

Blue Origin says the stage is not required for its lunar lander architecture, although it could support sustained operations around the Moon.

The company also unveiled Northstar, a booster engine it says has half the parts and 30 percent less mass than its BE-4, while increasing thrust from 640,000 to 715,000 pounds-force.

The memo said the team had "found and fixed the issue that caused the anomaly," without elaborating. Limp said in August that the problem "originated at the main oxygen valve on one of the BE-4 engines" and that existing engines would need retrofitting, but did not explain how the valve failed or what changes were required. The Register asked Blue Origin for comment. ®

Atomicwork launches certified AI coworker marketplace for enterprise IT and security

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Information technology service management startup Atomicwork Inc. today announced the launch of an Artificial Intelligence Coworker Marketplace that will give enterprise IT and security teams access to prebuilt, certified AI coworkers that can work alongside employees.

The company said these agentic AI coworkers include verified roles and capabilities across cloud infrastructure, access management, security and device operations.

“Most AI-native ITSM vendors today don’t have much to show for their agents’ effectiveness — a listing means ‘this agent works,’ not ‘this AI Agent has real, verified coverage of the systems it needs to touch and has been tested and fine-tuned using real-world enterprise use cases’ which has made enterprises wary of agentic claims,” Cheif Executive and co-founder Vijay Rayapati told SiliconANGLE.

Founded in 2022, Atomicwork began with the premise that information technology and security teams face challenging, process-intensive work and long tasks that could be automated. To tackle these problems, the company evaluated its agentic coworkers against real enterprise use cases, giving organizations a faster way to deploy AI workers without developing or validating them from scratch.

The initial lineup of coworkers includes an enterprise operations AI coworker, along with a CloudOps engineer, AccessOps manager, SecOps manager and DeviceOps manager. These AI coworkers can work across enterprise systems including AWS, Microsoft Azure, Google Cloud Platform, Okta, Active Directory, Splunk and Google SecOps.

The company said every AI Coworker comes out of the box with the skills and integrations needed for its role, reducing the work IT teams would otherwise spend wiring up systems and validating procedures.

Every agent can be quickly tailored to internal infrastructure, company culture, specialized expertise and business logic. Using that, they can quickly generate and tackle large volumes of recurring operational work under the supervision of IT and security teams. The company added that administrators will retain full control of agent management, allowing them to decide what systems an agent can access, what actions it can perform and how those actions are governed.

The objective is not merely to provide a catalog of available agents with a card to explain their capabilities. Atomicwork’s certification verifies that each AI Coworker has the needed integrations and skill coverage for the role to get the work done before deployment.

Atomicwork said customers using access automation have already seen speed-ups, with applications that don’t require approval having a median turnaround of about 18 seconds, while those requiring approval have a turnaround of about 2.5 hours. In contrast, the industry standard, which can be heavily influenced by business maturity and the risk behind the application’s use, could take between a full day and three business days depending on the amount of automation behind the scenes.

The company said this marketplace represents the beginning of a broader plan to create an ecosystem of enterprise AI Coworkers, built and certified by Atomicwork. Third parties will also be able to contribute skills and integrations through its Model Context Protocol store and skill registry to enhance coworker capabilities.

“This launch is the first step toward a third-party AI Coworker marketplace, where partners publish their own coworkers and certify them against our enterprise bench,” said Rayapati.

TikTok&#8217;s For You page needs to change, says the EU

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The EU Commission says TikTok’s ‘rabbit hole’ effect can pose harm to users.﻿

The EU Commission says TikTok’s ‘rabbit hole’ effect can pose harm to users.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The European Commission claims TikTok broke its online platform rules by offering a recommendation algorithm that creates a “rabbit hole effect” on users. In an announcement on Friday, the regulators allege TikTok’s algorithm can harm users with “endless and repeated exposure” to content related to sadness, isolation, or body image.

The findings come months after the EU Commission declared that TikTok’s design is too “addictive.” While TikTok has implemented controls to mitigate the algorithm’s “rabbit hole effect,” the Commission says its preliminary findings suggest that they’re not effective. TikTok must now change the design of its For You feed, or else face fines of up to 6 percent of its global annual turnover. The platform is also facing pressure in the US, with lawmakers and a wave of lawsuits raising concerns about child safety.

Since enacting the Digital Services Act in 2023 — a set of rules that hold tech companies legally accountable for their impact on users— the EU Commission has targeted several companies for allegedly violating the regulations, including X, Meta, Temu, and AliExpress. As reported by Politico, this is the first time the Commission has cited a recommendation algorithm as the reason for a breach.

“Every time a user scrolls or likes a video on TikTok, the algorithm serves more of the same leading users down rabbit holes they cannot easily escape from,” Henna Virkkunen, the EU Commission’s executive vice president of tech sovereignty, security, and democracy, says in the press release. “Today’s preliminary findings show again that TikTok needs to step up its efforts to protect users’ well-being, including those of minors.”
