Here's the Average Social Security Benefit at Age 70

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Waiting until 70 maximizes your benefit through delayed retirement credits, but the math on whether it pays off depends on your health and longevity.

The average Social Security benefit for people aged 70 is $2,274.68 per month as of December 2025, the most recent data available from the Social Security Administration (SSA). This is the age when you qualify for your maximum checks. Credits stop accruing once your 70th birthday arrives, so there's no financial incentive to wait any longer to claim.

The SSA bases your benefit on an average of your wages across your 35 highest-earning years. It adjusts your earlier wages for inflation and runs them through the primary insurance amount (PIA) formula. Your checks are designed to replace about 40% of your pre-retirement income, a share that's well short of what most retirees need.

At age 70, the average benefit among men is $2,529.62, while the average for women is $2,024.08. That's a gap of $505.54 per month, which often reflects historical differences in average career length and lifetime earnings between the genders. Across the whole age-70 population, that monthly average amounts to about $27,296.16 per year.

The SSA adds a delayed retirement credit of 8% for each year you wait past your full retirement age (FRA) -- age 67 for most workers today. For a worker whose FRA is 67, those who max out delayed retirement credits until age 70 earn a 24% increase. A worker with an FRA of 66 can earn up to a 32% increase for delaying until 70.

Waiting until 70 to collect a larger check usually makes the most sense if you're in good health or have a family history of longevity. The break-even age for this choice is roughly 82 to 83. This is the point where the total lifetime benefits you receive from claiming later equal the amount you would have collected by starting earlier. At age 65, men are expected to live about 18 more years, and women about 21 more years, according to the SSA.

Higher earners also often delay to secure a larger survivor benefit. If the higher-earning spouse dies first, their monthly benefit becomes the survivor's payment.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027. This adjustment adds about $82 a month on top of the average age-70 benefit. Because the SSA figures in the table above are from late 2025, they don't reflect this increase yet. Future adjustments can change these amounts depending on inflation trends.

Understanding these averages helps you determine if your own expected benefit will meet your retirement spending needs or if you'll need to rely more heavily on other income sources.

The age-70 average outlined above reflects everyone who is currently 70 and receiving benefits. It doesn't tell us anything about the average age at sign-up among this group. Many 70-year-olds applied earlier, and their benefits may include early claiming benefit reductions.

It's possible for seniors who wait until age 70 to apply for Social Security to take home much larger checks than the averages above, though this depends on their income history. Of course, waiting to apply means you'll need another way to cover your expenses until you're ready to sign up for benefits.

This might not be a problem if you plan to remain in the workforce for a while or if you've built up a large nest egg of your own. But if you don't have other income sources available, claiming at 70 might not be a viable option for you.

That doesn't mean you have to sign up for checks immediately, though. Even waiting a few months can permanently increase your checks and grow your lifetime benefit.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

GlobalFoundries VP details FDX Fusion technology, sporting 7nm-class performance without the need for EUV tools — company targets 2030 for high-volume manufacturing, with planar transistors in tow

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Eight years after GlobalFoundries abandoned the development of its 7nm FinFET technology, the company is preparing to return to 7nm-class performance, this time by going via a different route. Its FDX Fusion process, built on FD-SOI (fully depleted silicon-on-insulator) technology, promises 7nm-class performance using advanced planar transistors, with no EUV lithography necessary.

In an interview with Tom's Hardware Premium, GlobalFoundries' Sudipto Bose, vice president of the FD-SOI business unit, shared new details about its FDX Fusion technology, including its 8nm-class logic density, advanced planar transistors, manufacturing costs, and plans to begin commercial production in 2028. The company also explained how it intends to compete with conventional FinFET technologies and why it believes FD-SOI has a future beyond the 22nm-class generation. Some elements of this interview transcript have been edited for flow and clarity.

Sudipto Bose: I have been at GlobalFoundries (GF) for a little bit over six years, and I have been in the industry for 25+ years. I spent the rest of my career at Texas Instruments (TI), so it's really two jobs that I have done: TI and GF. Half and half engineering and business. I spent a lot of my business days in the automotive segment. This was what I was doing at GF when I joined, and now I have the FDX product line role.

Sudipto Bose: A little bit on the history of FDX at GF. GF has been investing in FD-SOI for a long time. It started [these efforts] before I joined. So I would say almost eight years now. And a lot of the investment thesis that drove us towards FDX Fusion is based on the traction that we have in the market today, right? We have quite a few customers who are ramping up. And I say quite a few; this is like high double-digit customers in the nineties that are ramping up right now.

Anton Shilov: What specific types of chips and devices is FDX Fusion designed for? Which application will benefit most from its capabilities?

Sudipto Bose: FDX Fusion will try to enhance the applications in which we play today. What do I mean by that? If you look at our current customers, they are designing chips, everything to do with connectivity. When I say connectivity, think of Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, cellular, satcom, all of that. So that is one class of customers.

There's another class of customers who are designing processors at the edge, so these are MCUs (microcontroller units) with some level of inference sitting inside. Think of customers like Alif Semiconductors. So that is another class: MCUs at the edge.

Then in automotive, we have a disproportionate exposure to radar, and this is because of the eighty-one gigahertz performance that FDX brings to the table. So we will engage with them as they try to enhance their portfolio. We also have an automotive MCU, but radar is where you will see a lot of press releases that we have done.

Last but not least, we also have exposure in the data center segment. This also goes back to the fT/fmax, the high-performance RF that we have, which is a good candidate for designing TIAs and clock recovery circuits.

Anton Shilov: By applications inside data centers, what exactly do you mean? CPUs?

Sudipto Bose: Not CPUs. It is in the connectivity space. So, if you think about GF's focus, we are very focused on two things in data centers: power and connectivity. So in the connectivity space, we have silicon photonics, or turning electrons into photons. But then when you receive them, you have the optical diode, again, a GF silicon photonics technology. After that, you need circuits that will amplify those weak electrical signals and drive them to the compute chip. This circuit, which amplifies the weak electrical signals, is right now being done by GF silicon-germanium, but once FDX Fusion is there, we'll offer them both [as] options. And so, to be very specific to your answer, these are data/signal paths inside the data center that feed into CPUs.

GlobalFoundries' FDX Fusion is a new manufacturing technology that promises 8nm-class logic density and 7nm-class power and performance without EUV lithography or FinFET transistors, which greatly reduces design costs and manufacturing costs at the cost of significantly lower transistor density compared to 7nm-class nodes.

Instead, the process relies on advanced planar fully depleted silicon-on-insulator (FD-SOI) transistors, strained silicon substrates, and aggressive optical patterning techniques to achieve transistor dimensions comparable to those of conventional 8nm-class FinFET nodes.

FDX Fusion also retains support for adaptive body biasing and combines high-density digital logic with RF, analog, and embedded memory capabilities. The technology will be manufactured using deep ultraviolet (DUV) lithography, although its tighter gate and metal pitches will likely require extensive multipatterning for the most critical layers.

Anton Shilov: What are the key innovations of FDX Fusion compared to predecessors and other nodes that are currently used?

Sudipto Bose: The key things that we're improving from the current-generation FDX, the first one is fT/fmax, this is 500 GHz, or 0.5 THz fT/fmax. This helps the first segment that I talked about, which is connectivity. This helps automotive radar, and this definitely helps the data center segment as they try to improve data speeds. So this is not really competing with silicon germanium. Think of it as silicon germanium at the very high end, and FDX in the 100 – 200 Gb per lambda space. That is one. The second one is the inference at the edge. So we can call it physical AI, and we look at physical AI as sense, think, act, and connect. But the kind of feature set we are putting in starts with improving the digital gate density. This is 2.2 times more dense than the current generation FDX (22FDX and 22FDX+).

Sudipto Bose: We are putting in a lot of flavors of eNVM, a lot of flavors of SRAM, because in edge AI, and in fact for any AI, the real game is how much memory you can pack without increasing the cost. So we are adding a lot of flavors of those. The way we architected FDX Fusion is that we set a goal of how much performance we can deliver to our customer base without increasing their operating expenses.

If you look at classic 7nm, 6nm, they require EUV tools. The mask set is costly; the tool chain is costly. The FDX Fusion platform that we've built isn't going to use EUV tools. It is also based on planar technology. So there are two advantages to this. One is that we can support a much more competitive mask set price compared to 6nm. We can also offer [customers] a lot more flexibility in terms of cycle time and in how many places we manufacture, because FDX Fusion does not need EUV tools.

So, to summarize the answer? We are trying to improve RF and analog performance, digital gate density, and the classes of memory that we offer. What applications are we trying to target? It is connectivity, edge AI, high-frequency mmWave, automotive radar, and definitely applications inside data centers.

Anton Shilov: You just mentioned transistor density. The press release says that it has more than twice the transistor density of the original 22FDX process, but you never actually published a transistor density number for this. Could you share any numbers?

Sudipto Bose: I will share a number with you. But I will also give you a little bit of context on how our customers look at our technology portfolio. If you look at the current generation, the one that we are manufacturing today, the one where I said we have 90+ customers ramping. Not designing, ramping. This basically comes from the flexibility of FD-SOI. You have the box, the depleted channel, and you can use body bias. So we have customers who are able to achieve 16nm-class power and performance with 22FDX. What do I mean by 16nm-class power and performance?

It essentially means that although our gate density is not as close to 16mm, when you look at the system-level requirements like CPU clock, but think of it as TOPS per watt, we are matching 16nm; in some cases, we are matching 12nm-class.

[...] If I were sitting in a time machine and went back seven years, instead of calling it 22FDX, we could have called it 16FDX, right? Or we could have called it 16nm-class FDX. So with that analogy, if you come to FDX Fusion, our gate density of FDX Fusion will be exactly the same as the industry's 8nm standard today.

It is 14 million gates per square millimeter; that is the density.

Note: Assuming he uses a two-input NAND gate as its reference, each gate contains four transistors, which translates to 56 MTr/mm², which is comparable to what Samsung's 8nm-class nodes use.

Sudipto Bose: Now, why do we call it 7nm-class? Or we actually thought of calling it 6nm-class, but then we said, let's wait for some silicon before we go too far ahead. It is because digital gate density is one aspect of a solution. It defines your physical size, the die size, and that is great. But then you need power and performance. And with the adaptive body bias (ABB) capability that FD-SOI has, and with the standard cell metallization innovations that we have brought in FDX Fusion, once a device is done and you again look at TOPS per watt, it should be equivalent, if not better, than what 7nm can achieve. That is our product target. So for a customer, the value it brings [customers] is: 'I do not have to use an EUV tool line. I do not have to use a mask set that costs many millions of dollars.'

I have something which costs a few millions of dollars, has great cycle time, but at the same time, I am getting TOPS per watt, which is similar to or better than 7nm. The die size might be bigger, but again, it should not be no bigger than what 8nm is today. And that is the messaging to the industry; that is why we say 7nm-class instead of saying, 'Hey, this is 7nm density.' We are trying to reflect what our customers have told us. That in the edge world, in the physical AI world, the size of the die is one aspect, but if the die does not meet the power requirement of the application, then the whole system will not function. So that is the push.

Anton Shilov: That is perhaps why you actually called the technology FDX Fusion instead of using some fancy number.

Sudipto Bose: Yes, because the moment we put a number [out], people put us in a box. And we want to give customers the optionality of, 'Listen, you want to design a transimpedance amplifier using the fT/fmax that we have? That's an analog circuit. No one cares about digital gate density. Go build it.'

'You want to build a simple MCU, which is just SRAM and IOs? We have 14 million gates per square millimeter. Go build it.' 'You want to build a mixed-signal design like a single-chip radar, Wi-Fi, a satcom digital beamformer? Here are the few other knobs that you can turn to get the best PPA or the best system performance that you need.'

That is why we chose the name Fusion; we are trying to imply [that] we give you many options. It is the combination of all the good things the world needs to design the physical AI dream that we have put in front of ourselves, like the whole stack concept of 'sense, think, act, and connect'. Let us see if it sticks. I think it is a bold move for us to go against the grain of the way the foundry industry typically names its platforms, but we feel pretty strong about it.

Anton Shilov: So, you shared the gate density number, but could you maybe also share things like contacted gate pitch, metal pitch as well?

Sudipto Bose: In time. The reason I mention that is there is a little bit of IP involved. We want to get the first silicon out, engage with a few customers before we publish the CPP and the M(etal) layers. […] We are pushing the limits of patterning and coloring as well as gate oxide engineering to get the best CPP and metal pitches, which will enable this level of density of gates. That is, I would say in terms of R&D dollars just at a very high level, 50% of R&D dollars is on the transistor itself because we are doing strained substrates. There are multiple techniques we are using to improve the PFET and NFET performance. The other 50% is all on CPP and metal pitches.

Anton Shilov: So, are those contacted gate pitches and metal pitches comparable to conventional 7nm class FinFET process technologies?

Anton Shilov: When we talk about 8nm, basically speaking, there is only one company that actually produces chips using an 8nm-class technology. That is Samsung Foundry. TSMC and Intel Foundry do not have 8nm-class technologies. So I suppose that we should compare it to Samsung's 8nm node, right?

Anton Shilov: When it comes to power consumption improvements versus 22FDX and 22FDX+, how much lower power can we get using the new FDX Fusion node?

Sudipto Bose: It depends on the application, but I understand your question. So if you just normalize with [iso performance and iso power], I am giving you an answer with adaptive body bias included. If you consider current-generation FDX as the base point, the goal of FDX Fusion is to get 30% higher performance and 40% lower power. Again, it is really application-driven [in] how low you can go. But if you take the standard core, our goal is to get at least a 20% improvement.

Anton Shilov: What types of chips do you expect to benefit most from the FDX Fusion technology? You mentioned connectivity, edge AI, high RF, automotive radar, and connectivity applications inside data centers. What about application-specific AI accelerators, because you mentioned edge inference?

Sudipto Bose: From the STAC — sense, think, act, and connect — framework point of view, let me give you an example. Think of a humanoid, or a car — major applications that need to see. It needs to see and then infer what the environment is like. So the class of chips that do this vision processing at the edge to infer the surroundings will definitely be supported with FDX Fusion. The class of chips that will hear, do voice recognition, and, from voice recognition, draw conclusions, we definitely want to support [them]. Now, at the data center right now, as I said, our play is connectivity. Just translating optical to electrical signals.

If a customer wants to use a chiplet, which has a lot of memory, think of analog in-memory compute along with an inference engine, by all means we will support [such applications]. We will work with customers to see how we can play in that compute ecosystem in the data center. But in terms of pure focus, the first 10 customers that we are engaged with are either at the edge, or they are in the connectivity piece inside the data center.

Note: For those of our readers who are not familiar with ABB, this is a technique that dynamically adjusts a transistor's threshold voltage by changing the voltage applied to the transistor body. Forward body bias reduces threshold voltage to improve performance, while reverse body bias increases it to reduce power leakage. This enables chips to optimize performance and power consumption according to workload requirements. ABB is one of the key advantages of the FD-SOI technology.

Anton Shilov: Since you mentioned this, does FDX Fusion retain the active body biasing capabilities of its predecessors?

Sudipto Bose: It is an inseparable part of the platform, yes. We will launch this platform in a way that customers can use it.

Anton Shilov: You also mentioned makers of wafer fab equipment. Will FDX Fusion use existing manufacturing equipment at the Dresden facility, or will it require substantial investments in new lithography, deposition, etching, and metrology tools?

Sudipto Bose: It will require new tools. Substantial is a relative term. If you see the amount of money being invested in semiconductors, the term 'substantial' has changed. We are going to get new tools, which is why we combined the FDX Fusion announcement event with the topping off of the Sprint expansion project. That's why we had all the luminaries from the German and the European government. There will be tool investment; we will be buying tools from the vendors. How much of it is going to be litho versus etching? Time will tell, but for sure we need new tools compared to what we have today.

Anton Shilov: But will you essentially need to completely re-equip the Dresden facility to use FDX Fusion? What percentage of the tools you have can be reused for FDX Fusion?

Sudipto Bose: We can get back to you on that exact answer because I do want to give you a proper answer, rather than a high-level answer. But I will tell you where the targets are. With the front end, the transistor fabrication tool chain, my target is to reuse as much as we can.

Anton Shilov: You also mentioned that you plan to begin manufacturing FDX Fusion in 2028. But does this refer to risk production, initial customer production, or high volume manufacturing?

Sudipto Bose: Initial customer production.

Anton Shilov: When do you expect the technology to reach mature yields and meaningful production volumes?

Sudipto Bose: I will give you my target, and then the industry will define it because we are a part of a bigger ecosystem. 2030 is when we want to get to high-volume manufacturing with FDX Fusion. But again, it depends on the first applications and the first few customers.

Anton Shilov: Speaking of customers, you probably already have customers who are committed to production, haven't you?

Sudipto Bose: We have customers who are committed to building a chip. Of course, we have to deliver to their specs before they go to production.

Anton Shilov: I suppose the customers that you mentioned in the press release like BMW, Bosch NXP, Siemens, and Tesla, they are all plan to build chips on this technology.

Sudipto Bose: […] They have been pushing us for the last few years to do something like this, and they have given us requirements. Now we are doing it, and we have to show them what we can [deliver]. So there is a lot of push that we have received from the industry, from these companies. When I say these companies, the supply chain that leads into these companies to deliver something which will work at the edge. So we are racing against time to show them something that will meet the specs.

Anton Shilov: GlobalFoundries plans to release the initial FDX Fusion PDK in mid-2027. But when do you expect complete digital, analog, and RF design flows to become available from the three major EDA vendors?

Sudipto Bose: Yeah. I wish I could tell you… Right now we are in discussion with all the major primes, so we are in the request for information/request for quotation (RFI/RFQ) phase, but we have started discussions with the major EDA vendors. It has been almost six to nine months now.

Anton Shilov: But you do expect all the leading EDA vendors to support FDX Fusion in eight to nine months?

Sudipto Bose: Of course. We are going to engage with the whole ecosystem that we have, and once [the PDK is out], we have to support a proper flow not just for chip design, but also IPs that are required and everything else around it.

Anton Shilov: So how extensive will the initial FDX Fusion IP ecosystem be? Will customers have access to silicon-proven standard cell libraries, SRAM compilers, embedded non-volatile memory, interface IPs, maybe analog and radio frequency building blocks before 2028?

Sudipto Bose: Yes. In the first two years, we are going to engage with pilot customers. Think of these as early adopters. We have early adopters listed, and we will design standard cell, I/Os, and SRAM [...] needed for everything, so we will have those. But then the complex IPs, like PCIe Gen 4/5 or LPDDR6 or a DisplayPort, will be dictated by the first few applications that will ramp. FDX Fusion for us is the beginning of a journey. We have been on the journey of 22FDX for almost eight years. I envision FDX Fusion to be more than that. Even on our current platforms, we keep enhancing our IP portfolio. So I expect us to be adding IPs to FDX Fusion over the next ten years.

Anton Shilov: GlobalFoundries also highlighted its ARC and MIPS processor IP portfolios and RISC-V expertise as part of the FDX Fusion offering. Do you plan to develop processor cores and AI accelerators specifically optimized for FDX Fusion? IPs specifically optimized for FDX Fusion? Or will you primarily port existing IP to the new process?

Sudipto Bose: MIPS is an internal customer and a partner, and we will engage with them, and they will launch a platform for physical AI that is fine-tuned, in a combination of what they have and what FDX Fusion has. ARC and MIPS are key pieces of the strategy. Having an internal customer like that just enhances our development cycle by 5X because now they can start developing fast turns of test chips and give us feedback. We did not have this capability in the past. Now we do. Once they are ready, they will start announcing stuff.

Anton Shilov: So essentially, you will both port the existing IPs to the new node, but also MIPS will likely develop something brand new specifically for FDX Fusion.

Anton Shilov: How will the manufacturing complexity and cost per transistor of FDX Fusion compare to 22FDX+, your 12nm FinFET technology, and conventional 8nm FinFET technology?

Sudipto Bose: This would be the most advanced planar transistor that the world has seen, the most advanced non-EUV-based manufacturing line that the world has seen. So, I would say significantly more complex than our current 22nm-class FD-SOI line. More complex than what we have on the 12nm FinFET line. We are working with every tool vendor that you can think of as part of this program, and we have given them the challenges. It goes back to your question about our contacted gate pitch and metal pitch. So, we have had multiple workshops with them over the last few quarters, and each one of them is proposing a tool recipe that they think will get this to a nice and solid yield.

Sudipto Bose: So, I guess the answer to your question is: firstly, it is definitely complex, and secondly, we are really using the entire ecosystem available to us to come up with tool configurations that will deliver this. All of this is going to happen in Dresden. We have to be competitive, so price is always dictated by the market. Our goal is to have a running cost that is significantly better than a 7nm or a 6nm class of design (due to planar transistors and due to lack of EUV masks). After that, we will work with our customers to figure out what makes sense. But you are right, the value that we want to bring in is, 'dear customers, you do not have the same operating expenses load, meaning the cost of tape-out is less than 6nm.'

And you can do multiple tape-outs because this class of devices is not something where you can design one part and use it for 15 applications. You really need 15 devices for 15 applications. So, we definitely enable that. Then, when it comes to manufacturing cost, it is a discussion with our customers. We have various business models, but the goal is to make sure it is a win-win. At the end of the day, the lower the cost we are, the more flexibility we can provide to our customers. The non-EUV path is one of the things that was dictated by our decision that we must keep this a cost-competitive platform.

Anton Shilov: So, cost-wise you want to be competitive, but it's likely comparable to 8nm.

Sudipto Bose: I will have a better feel once we are complete with what we call our M-cycles inside the fab. But I would say my internal target is that we have to be better than that.

Anton Shilov: GlobalFoundries describes upcoming technology as its 1st Generation of FDX Fusion. Does this mean that you envision subsequent generations with high transistor density and performance? Maybe you can share some preliminary targets or timelines for the next node?

Sudipto Bose: We have listed quite a few innovation steps that we want to enable on this platform. The FDX Fusion definition is based on the ones we believe we are more confident that we will be able to deliver in the timeframe that we have said. We have kept a few things in the innovation lab, and they are pushing the boundaries of a few things like eNVM, EDA routing, chip folding, 2.5D integration, which, once they mature, will define what the next version of FDX Fusion will look like. I do not have a timeline for the next version of FDX Fusion, though.

As we proceed through the development phase, I would say early 2028 to mid-2028 is when I expect to have a better view of when the next version will be launched. And this is also based on feedback we will get from customers. Because what we have right now is our first silicon, which is running in the lab, which we will show to customers. They will give us a lot of feedback. I am sure we will get a few things right, and at the same time, we will get a few things not so right. And that feedback, along with a few of the innovation topics I mentioned, will dictate what the next generation is going to look like. Stay tuned!

Anton Shilov: One of the reasons why I aksed about next-generation nodes is partly because TSMC typically shares its process technology roadmaps with new nodes well before it releases early PDKs for these nodes. Given that FDX Fusion is now GF's most advanced logic process, with capabilities comparable to 8nm-class technologies (i.e., more advanced than 22FDX or bulk 12nm FinFET), how frequently does GlobalFoundries plan to introduce new process technologies, and how far in advance will it disclose its roadmap?

Sudipto Bose: I would say, we are doing advanced development across the board. If you look at the work we are doing on silicon photonics, on packaging, on power transistors like GaN, RF GaN, Power GaN, these are all pretty advanced. But yes, you are right: in what the world understands as a classic, let's say, high-density process, FDX Fusion is the most advanced. We typically announce or update the industry about our roadmap every year at the GlobalFoundries Technology Symposium. Like this year at GTS, we announced FDX Fusion. So, I would say stay tuned for the GTS sessions that we have, and typically every year we do it in September or October.

Anton Shilov: CEA-Leti describes FDX Fusion as a single-digit FD-SOI node. How closely is this new technology related to CEA-Leti's own research in 10nm and 7nm with their SOI nodes?

Sudipto Bose: The platform that we have right now is the outside of the substrate engineering work that we are doing with Soitec and CEA-Leti; the rest of it is GF inherent IP at this point in time. So we are bringing in the knowledge that we have from current-generation FDX, the knowledge that we have from the work that we did in 7nm before we stopped it, the work that we have done on 12nm. So it is bringing in all the innovations across all the GF fabs to figure out what is the best that we can offer. Does it mean that we will not look at what CEA-Leti has in the future? I do not have an answer right now. But for now, FDX Fusion is a very GF innovation-driven effort.

Anton Shilov: So you did not use pre-competitive IP from CEA-Leti to develop FDX Fusion? Is it a completely internally developed node?

Anton Shilov: The press release mentions innovations in substrate engineering along with endorsements from CEA-Leti and Soitec. What exactly does that involve, and how does it improve transistor performance? What role do Soitec and CEA-Leti play in developing FDX's strained silicon substrates, and how close is this technology to mass production?

Sudipto Bose: GlobalFoundries is working with CEA-Leti and Soitec under the FAMES program to develop strained silicon substrates for FDX Fusion. The goal is to improve charge carrier mobility and, consequently, transistor performance. Since FD-SOI technology relies on specialized silicon-on-insulator wafers supplied by Soitec, the three organizations are developing ways to introduce strain into these substrates and make the technology suitable for high-volume manufacturing. The approach has already demonstrated its viability in the laboratory, and the partners are now refining the manufacturing process to prepare it for commercial production.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Elegoo M1 Ultra has a lot going for it, but it is not a standalone system. You’ll need to combine it with a powerful laptop to process the scanning data into anything useful. The good news it can be tethered via Wi-Fi.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

The M1 Ultra is Elegoo’s first entry into the 3D scanner market. It is a feature-rich scanner with five scanning modes, four blue light laser modes, and a NIR binocular structured light mode. Most importantly, the scanner has a 3-in color touch screen and long battery life. It even includes a spare rechargeable battery, so you’re always prepared.

The scanner can’t run without a fairly high end PC or Mac for processing power. It will need to be tethered to the laptop with a UBS-C cable or connected via Wi-Fi. I was able to get about five feet away from my subject in a harsh RF environment. I didn’t test it further than that, so it may go farther. The cable connection provides the fastest connectivity, making tethered scanning worth the hassle.

The scanner’s output rivals any of the scanners I’ve tested, but it did require a little extra work to get there. At a current price of $1,599 at Elegoo, the M1 Ultra has features not found in other scanners at this price point.

The Elegoo M1 Ultra comes with a nice hard case for travel, with a padded interior to protect the scanner, glass calibration board, spare battery, NIR filters, and a USB-C cable. Also included are a lens cleaning cloth and a packet of marker dots. Noticeably absent is a turntable for scanning smaller objects. (We borrowed a turntable from another scanner during this test.)

The Elegoo M1 Ultra weighs 511g (1.12lbs) with its battery installed. It has a rubber grip below the screen and is nicely balanced. Users with large hands may find their thumbs blocking the screen.

The onboard screen can be used to set the scanning parameters, start and stop scans, and monitor the scans as they progress. The 3-inch screen is small, but the UI is laid out perfectly. When setting parameters for a scan, the touch screen takes precedence over the tethered computer if you’re starting the scan from the scanner itself.

The business end of the scanner has a series of blue, white, and infrared LEDs, blue lasers, and cameras. The included NIR filters are needed when scanning outdoors or in harsh light conditions. The battery and the spare can be charged using a USB-C cable when unmounted, and can be charged from the computer when operating the scanner in USB mode. The battery life is excellent. Almost all of our testing was done with one charge of the battery and its spare.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Calibrating the Elegoo M1 Ultra is fairly easy given its light weight and the easy-to-follow software. Using the glass calibration board, the scanner is aimed at various angles and heights, following the directions on screen. The software shows an image of where the scanner should be and an image of the scanner as you move it. You have to match the two before moving on to the next spot. Scanner calibration can be a chore, but calibrating the M1 Ultra was simple.

Elegoo Scan can be downloaded from Elegoo’s website for Windows and Mac. The software works and looks like CrealityScan, which is a good thing since both make cleaning up individual scans and stitching multiple scans together fairly easy. The software does require a bunch of memory, with the more, the merrier. I tried running the software on an older PC laptop which met the minimum requirements, except it had only 8 GB of RAM, and it was not enough.

Elegoo recommends a PC with Windows 10/11 (64-bit), an Intel Core i7 (13th Gen) or higher, an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4060 or higher (8 GB VRAM), and 32 GB or more of RAM. For Mac, they recommend macOS 14 or later, with an Apple M4 or later, and 32 GB or more of RAM. Minimum specs for both are 16gb of RAM, with Windows 10/11 (64-bit), Intel Core i7 (7th Gen), and an Nvidia GPU (6 GB VRAM) for PC, and macOS 14 or later, with an Apple M2 CPU.

The Elegoo M1 Ultra has 4 blue laser scanning modes: 34 crossed laser lines for quick scanning, 7 parallel lines for finer details, 1 line for scanning deeper, more difficult-to-capture holes, and markerless. The fewer the lines, the more time the scans take. I should point out that the turntable in the photo was borrowed from the Creality Pika Scanner, which we reviewed previously.

One of my favorite use cases is scanning an item for import into a 3D printing project I am working on. I scanned a cheap 895 brushed motor commonly found in drills. The motor required several scans, and the M1 Ultra seemed to have trouble capturing the shinier parts of the object. Ultimately, I captured two scans that had enough data to be useful. The software has an automatic alignment tool which does a good job in most cases. In this case, it was baffled by the geometry. The manual alignment worked perfectly.

Manual alignment requires adding markers matching points on both images. You do not have to be super exact with the placement. The software will smooth out any overlaps.

Part of the mesh processing is hole filling. This also has an automatic and manual mode. Since the automatic mode was going to fill in the screw holes and cooling holes, I had to fill in the holes manually by clicking on them. This is a little tedious, but it worked well.

After hole filling, smoothing, and simplifying, I was able to export the file as an STL and open it in Tinkercad.

The size, and hole alignment were perfect for integrating the motor into a project.

This bust of Michelangelo’s David came with an early Creality scanner and has become my main test file. It took three scans for me to get a good model, which was a recurring theme with the Elegoo M1 Ultra.

Fortunately in this case, the auto alignment function worked perfectly.

Even with multiple scans, there were still a few holes to fill. One of these can be seen in the finished model in the smooth, flat area above David’s left ear.

Overall, the model generated is one of the best I’ve seen, and it printed extremely well once exported. The flaw at the bottom of the print was a bed adhesion issue rather than a problem with the model.

Since I could not find a willing human victim…I mean, subject…to scan, I tried using the NIR mode to scan Clyde, our family’s favorite stuffed Clydesdale. This is a cautionary tale because 3D scanners in general have a tough time scanning black objects, as they absorb light rather than reflecting it.

The scans weren’t bad, but they did leave big holes where the mane and tail were too dark to read.

I was able to do a much better scan of Ali the Alligator. In NIR mode, the scanner is capable of 30fps. Even with the slower frame rate, the scanner was able to quickly capture Ali in two scans, without a ton of multiple passes. Each time you go over the same spot on an object, it’s possible to generate garbage data that has to be removed in the meshing process. The two scans align together automatically, and the finished model is pretty darn good.

The scanner does feature color texture mapping. Other than the mouth, where a large hole needed to be filled, the color mapping was spot on.

The Elegoo M1 Ultra is a good scanner that has a good feature set for the price. The light weight, onboard screen, long battery life, and stable Wi-Fi connection make it a joy to use.

The ElegooScan software is fairly intuitive. It makes calibrating the scanner and turning the raw scans into usable models fairly easy. The Elegoo M1 Ultra did require multiple scans to get good coverage of objects, while other scanners I’ve tested could do it in one pass. The added complexity takes up a good deal of time and requires a fair amount of computer power, especially when cleaning up and aligning multiple scans. ElegooScan has the tools to make it work. Even with a pretty good model, you’ll likely also need a CAD program or other design tools to make the best use of the scans.

If you’d like to save a few bucks and get a still excellent scanner, check out the Creality Pika, which is currently $699. If you want a scanner with a little more horsepower, check out the Creality Sermoon S1, which retails for $2,069. Creality also makes the Sermoon P1, which can run completely untethered and retails for $3,299.

Denise Bertacchi is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware US, covering 3D printing. Denise has been crafting with PCs since she discovered Print Shop had clip art on her Apple IIe. She loves reviewing 3D printers because she can mix all her passions: printing, photography, and writing.

Black Panther 2 is still Marvel's best passing of the torch – so I hope Shuri gets to shine in Avengers: Doomsday

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

On the road to Avengers: Doomsday, Black Panther: Wakanda Forever remains Marvel's best passing of the torch

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Shuri was never supposed to be the Black Panther. In 2020, Chadwick Boseman, who portrayed T'Challa with so much regal gravitas, died at the age of 43. In the terrible aftermath, Black Panther: Wakanda Forever was released – a beautiful entry in the MCU that functions both as a deeply loving tribute to Boseman and an emotional passing of the torch to Letitia Wright's Shuri.

It's this context that makes Black Panther: Wakanda Forever such an extraordinary accomplishment. Not only is it a genuinely thrilling chapter in the MCU, but it also pulls off the tricky task of handing over the mantle in a way that feels respectful and natural to the characters involved, while honoring the real Boseman. It doesn't shy away from this reality, instead choosing to tell a story that focuses heavily on Shuri's raw grief and her complicated journey to stepping up as her nation's protector.

Now, Shuri is set to return in Avengers: Doomsday, standing alongside other Marvel titans like Chris Hemsworth's Thor and the original X-Men. I just hope that, among the stacked cast, Shuri gets her chance to shine – because she's earned it.

Wakanda Forever begins with T'Challa dying from a mystery illness. Despite her super-genius intellect, Shuri is powerless against this invisible enemy and unable to synthesize a new heart-shaped herb, and so T'Challa dies. Shuri, who idolized her big brother, is devastated – and, after a time-skip of a year, she is still struggling with her all-encompassing grief, which is making her as angry as she is heartbroken.

Welcome to Road to Doomsday, GamesRadar+'s weekly MCU rewatch series. Over 15 weeks, we'll be revisiting each Marvel movie and TV show on Disney's list of essential movies to watch ahead of Avengers: Doomsday, sharing our thoughts upon rewatching and deciphering what we can learn about the next Marvel mega-event from each entry. Why not relive some MCU highlights and watch along with us?

It's this raw emotion that makes Shuri's journey to becoming a fully fledged hero so compelling. Shuri, very understandably, does not want to become the Black Panther. It doesn't even cross her mind. Instead, she has the incredibly human reaction of simply wanting to grieve. But in the Marvel universe, nobody has any breathing space for long, and the arrival of Namor and the threat of CIA intervention over Vibranium pushes Shuri back into the fray. It's when the situation spirals out of control, and Namor kills her mother, Queen Ramonda, that Shuri finally returns to attempting to reconstruct the heart-shaped herb.

As a showdown with Killmonger in the Ancestral Plane demonstrates, though, Shuri is not solely motivated by an altruistic desire to be a hero: she wants revenge. Her anger and pain at the loss of T'Challa have been exacerbated by the murder of her mother, resulting in a furious grief that threatens to plunge Wakanda into an endless war with its underwater neighbours. It's a natural breaking point in her struggle to deal with so much loss, and sets Shuri's arc apart from the rise to greatness of other MCU heroes in its messy, intense vulnerability and pain.

Ultimately, though, Shuri, movingly encouraged by the spirit of Ramonda, chooses peace by sparing Namor's life and putting an end to the fighting between Wakanda and Talokan. This triumphant finish clears the way, allowing Shuri to finally process her grief – and even meet her nephew, T'Challa II. Her story in the film ends with a quiet moment on the beach, burning her mourning clothes, letting go of the painful past in order to face a more hopeful future.

Shuri's journey is not easy, and it's not one she even wanted – mirroring the awful real-life circumstances that led to the storyline. In this anguish is a beautiful evolution, powered by a standout performance from Wright, that brings Shuri from the fun, kid sister sidekick to the Black Panther herself. This passing of the torch is so effective because it truly means something, both within the MCU and in real life, and it gifts Shuri with boundless potential.

It was announced at SDCC that Shuri will soon be passing on the mantle herself, this time to T'Challa II. The Long Walk star David Jonsson will play the character, though how it will all work out is unclear at the moment, since Jonsson is much older than the young T'Challa II at the end of Wakanda Forever. ("In this next film, he grows up, comes of age, and we had to find an incredible actor," director Ryan Coogler shared on stage.)

This passing of the torch is so effective because it truly means something

Hopefully, then, Shuri gets her chance to shine in Avengers: Doomsday before surrendering the mantle. This is her first opportunity to truly spread her wings as the Black Panther after Wakanda Forever, and, with such a stacked cast, there's every risk Shuri might fall by the wayside – which would be a huge mistake, after how far she has come. She can be glimpsed in the trailers so far, though sadly not for very long.

Before Avengers: Doomsday, Spider-Man: No Way Home proved the best Marvel event movies put character first

But Wright may have already inadvertently let slip that she's part of the Avengers: Secret Wars cast, which would mean we have one more chance after Doomsday to see her in all her power before Jonsson takes over in Black Panther 3. What might happen in Secret Wars heavily depends on Doomsday, of course, but this duo of blockbuster event films offers the perfect chance to bring Shuri's MCU story to a meaningful close, paying off the emotional set-up of Wakanda Forever.

Shuri was never supposed to be the Black Panther, but she has more than proved how capable she is of being Wakanda's protector – and I hope Doomsday gives her the chance to truly show it.

Black Panther: Wakanda Forever is available to stream now on Disney Plus, ahead of the release of Avengers: Doomsday on December 18.

I keep forgetting to jump in Ocarina of Time remake, and two dungeons in I'm yearning for classic controls

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is a remake masterpiece, but takes one leap too far

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I'm on a roll as I enter Dodongo's Cavern, the fiery labyrinth and second dungeon in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake. Despite plenty of tweaks and changes, muscle memory of my previous playthroughs has kept me on task – adult wallet acquired, deku nuts on lock, Hylian Shield strapped on my back. Then, I walk forward a few steps towards ledges above flaming pools of lava and immediately faceplant, my poor little elfin boy immediately set aflame. Yes, Navi, I need some hearts.

Perhaps the biggest gameplay change in Ocarina of Time remake compared to the Nintendo 64 original is swapping the auto-jump to a manual button press. Previously, running toward an edge would have the green-garbed Link always jump from the furthest point possible. Now, I have to press a button to do it myself. Great. And why don't I rock up to my local pizza place to bake my own pie next time I order, too? While I see Super Mario 64 challenge runs completing that game without jumping at all, I've been doomed to tap the jump-button in the Zelda equivalent endlessly.

I'm exaggerating, but only a bit. There are actually loads of times in which the manual control of Link's hops, and how reactive all his moves are in a world that feels utterly teeming with life, helps this remake buzz with a new energy. Small layout changes to early areas add a smidge more naturalistic verticality to some zones, while scampering through towns and mantling ledges simply makes navigation of the overworld much more swish.

Still, it's in the more traditional Zelda dungeon exploration and challenges that I stumble. I'm so used to routing my way through these dangerous areas by pointing myself in the right direction and chaining together auto-jumps that I find myself forgetting or mistiming the input to go early or roll by mistake and end up going splat. This is very embarrassing to admit as a big platformer fan who usually wastes no time click-clacking the stick to do side somersaults up forbidden pathways. Something about having to press the jump button in Ocarina of Time is vexing me. And I'm far from the only one.

In part, it's because I have played these games before. These jumps are deeply familiar to me, designed without a manual jump button at all, only to now have the need foisted upon them. It's not like Elden Ring where the addition of a more natural jump compared to Dark Souls was incorporated into its level design approach from the ground-up.

While the position of, say, the pond with the platforms in Kokiri Village may have been adjusted, the sequence is the same as the original. Except, now, you have to press another button the whole time while doing it rather than thinking only about steering.

I'm being picky, and a bit of a grumbler – I do agree with Anna's 5-star Ocarina of Time remake review. This is an exceptional overhaul. But, it's not as if the concept of a jump-button was completely alien to the N64 development team – the original game was intentionally designed not to need a jump button. It's not like they just forgot to include it.

Sign up to our Switch 2 Spotlight newsletter to make sure you get the very latest news on Nintendo's new console and its games every week

In fact, I'd say more isn't always better – like a sloppy, delicious-looking burger with too many toppings to get your jaw around. For me, some of the joy of revisiting classic Zelda is the simplicity of control that allows depth elsewhere. It's not as if auto-jumping was an N64 limitation, the traversal mechanic having remained in the series all the way through to Breath of the Wild.

Many of the best Zelda games, as a result, make fantastic use of auto-jump without the need for manual jump at all – Wind Waker, Twilight Princess, all the rest. In Breath of the Wild it made sense with the design shift to the open world, freeform focused exploration, away from tight dungeon design. Ocarina of Time remake isn't so different that this fundamental control choice suddenly needs an overhaul.

I'm not fuming it's here, but I'm a bit baffled at the dedication to manual jump. If you're going to add it in, why not keep auto-jump as a toggle or additional option? After all, the Ocarina of Time remake dev team explicitly kept the classic jump-attack and rolling options alongside their modern control versions. Granted, some reworked sections now require manual jumping – climbing up a bit of wall into the Hyrule Castle gardens, for example, which I quite like – but why does that mean I also can't auto-jump when I run at an edge?

Largely, Ocarina of Time remake is a more well-considered overhaul than I had first feared. Over the last few years, I've been finding remakes broadly very hit and miss. Loads of small tweaks feel dialled in to perfection, from the exact amount of luscious detail to enemy animations making combat feel more reactive – all while respecting much of the core design that made Ocarina of Time such a classic. But, when I'm tumbling into the lava because I forgot that not all original design concepts are quite so revered as others at the altar of strapping on more game mechanics, well, it burns a little.

Time marches on. Check out our upcoming Switch 2 releases for more adventures on the horizon!

Naturally, given the time of year, there are plenty of holiday movies now streaming on Paramount+, but making my pick was easy as Paramount+ just added my personal favorite Christmas film of all time. If you're not quite ready for festive-themed streaming, however, I'm also highlighting an iconic Sylvester Stallone action-thriller and an overlooked WWII drama.

Don't waste your weekend scrolling through the more than 120 movies just added to Paramount+. Here are the three that need to be at the very top of your watchlist.

"It's a Wonderful Life" is practically mandatory viewing over the holiday season. This 1946 Frank Capra classic isn't just the best Christmas movie of all time, but one of the greatest films of the 20th century. Charming, warm and sentimental, it celebrates the true reason for the season in quite some style. It's a feel-good movie that almost every new holiday movie takes inspiration from; it's quite simply a timeless masterpiece.

James Stewart stars as George Bailey, a selfless man who has given up his own dreams to help his family and community. On Christmas Eve, he reaches rock bottom and considers suicide. However, his guardian angel, Clarence (Henry Travers), intervenes and shows George what a lesser place the world would be if he had never existed. Also starring Donna Reed and Lionel Barrymore, "It's a Wonderful Life" lives up to its sterling reputation.

Watch "It's a Wonderful Life" on Paramount+ now

Sylvester Stallone's John Rambo is one of the 1980s most iconic action heroes, which makes his debut, "First Blood," a little peculiar. Sequels would depict Rambo as an unstoppable killing machine, but in his first outing, he's a traumatized soldier, scarred by what he saw and what he did during the Vietnam War. In fact, Rambo kills only a single person in the entire movie, and even that death is typically classified as accidental.

Sign up to the weekly Tom's Streaming Guide to stay in the loop with the latest releases and top recommendations across your favorite streaming services. We’ve got you covered with a complete guide on what’s worth watching.

If you're looking for '80s action pulp, go for one of the sequels, but if you want a tense thriller, then stream "First Blood." In my books, it's the best Rambo film of the lot. In "First Blood," Rambo (Stallone) finds himself on the wrong side of an arrogant small-town sheriff (Brian Dennehy); their confrontation spirals into a manhunt, as the law attempts to capture Rambo. But this former Special Forces soldier is a tricky man to catch.

The above picks are legendary movies. While they're still exceptional in 2026, you may have seen them before. So, for my final option this week, I'm going for "Allied." Directed by Robert Zemeckis (the filmmaker behind "Cast Away" and "Contact"), this 2016 wartime romantic drama stars Brad Pitt and Marion Cotillard. It was a box office flop and received lukewarm reviews upon release, but I believe it's worthy of a second chance.

Opening in 1942, during World War II, operative Max Vatan (Pitt) is paired with a French Resistance fighter, Marianne Beauséjour (Cotillard). The two pose as a married couple as part of a plan to assassinate a German ambassador, only for their pretend romance to become real. They settle in London, get married, have a baby, and all seems well. That is, until Max is informed by his superiors that Marianne might just be a German spy.

Aston Villa have only just brought one player to the Midlands - but Real Madrid AND Paris Saint-Germain are circling

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Aston Villa are bracing themselves for Real Madrid and Paris Saint-Germain interest in one of their newest recruits.

Villa are in a strong position for the future, with Guillem Balague writing for the BBC this week that manager Unai Emery is close to agreeing a contract extension in the Midlands that will see him given even more responsibility over the long-term development of the club.

But the Europa League winners could well have to fend off interest in some of their newest stars, with more European giants circling for their new signings, after it was revealed earlier this week that Barcelona have interest in Johan Manzambi.

Emery oversaw a huge rebuild at Villa Park over the summer, with the likes of Emiliano Martinez, Ezri Konsa, Lucas Digne, Youri Tielemans, Morgan Rogers and Ollie Watkins all leaving the club.

With the spine of a successful side ripped from squad, the Basque has started strongly this term, bedding in replacements like Manzambi and Nicolas Jackson to keep Villa competing on four fronts.

One particularly impressive new signing, Zion Suzuki, has caught the eye for his elite distribution – and now, TEAMtalk are reporting that both Real and PSG are interested in the Japanese international.

The 24-year-old enjoyed a good World Cup and has been an outstanding replacement for Martinez in goal, despite big clubs having passed on signing him in the past.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Manchester United were linked with him at one point, while FourFourTwo understands that PSG have previously considered a move for Suzuki, only to pass up the opportunity.

Los Blancos are said to be working on a short-term extension for veteran glovesman Thibaut Courtois, but with the club looking to the future between the sticks, Suzuki has emerged as a serious target who could hold down the position for the next decade.

TEAMtalk have also noted Liverpool are monitoring the situation, given the uncertainty surrounding Alisson Becker's future.

Villa will, of course, be in no rush to sell a player that they have only just signed, with Emery keen to build around the new core that the club has assembled – and with the club back in the Champions League and upgrading their stadium, there is certainly hope that there isn't as much need to sell this group of players as there was with the last.

Arsenal and Manchester City are also named in the report as two clubs checking on the long-term viability of signing Suzuki.

The Japanese is worth €30 million, according to Transfermarkt. Villa travel to Manchester United this weekend as Premier League action continues.

He has written cover features for the mag on Mikel Arteta and Martin Odegaard, and is assisted by his cat, Rosie, who has interned for the brand since lockdown.

DOJ steps up its investigation into the Dobbs leak

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

This week, during the first half of the November argument session, the justices heard oral arguments in four cases and quickly declined a request from the Trump administration to intervene in a voting dispute in California. However, headlines about these developments were overshadowed by Tuesday’s midterm elections. The blue wave brought new significance to debates over court reform and when Justices Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas might retire.

Today, the justices will meet in a private conference to discuss cases and vote on petitions for review. Orders from today’s conference are expected on Monday at 9:30 a.m. EST.

On Thursday, the Supreme Court denied two requests for a stay of execution from Jesse Guardado, who was sentenced to death for the 2004 murder of Jackie Malone. Hours later, Guardado was executed in Florida.

On Wednesday, the justices heard argument in Guerrero v. Johnson, on the limited circumstances in which a state prisoner can return to federal court for a second round of post-conviction review. Here is the link to our Case Preview.

On Tuesday, the justices heard argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, a religious discrimination challenge brought by Catholic preschools in Colorado over the state’s requirement that all schools participating in its universal preschool program must admit everyone – including LGBTQ children and children with LGBTQ parents. Here are the links to our Case Preview and Argument Analysis.

On Monday, the justices heard argument in Maxwell v. Dinis, on a federal inmate’s ability to use a habeas statute, 28 U.S.C. § 2241, to contest the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ refusal to accelerate his transfer to a halfway house or home confinement. Here are the links to our Case Preview and Argument Analysis.

The justices also heard argument on Monday in Younge v. Fulton Judicial Circuit District Attorney’s Office, Georgia, on whether an affirmative defense may be asserted as a basis for summary judgment if not presented earlier in a pleading. Here are the links to our Case Preview and Argument Analysis.

Monday morning, the Trump administration returned to the Supreme Court’s interim docket, asking the justices to intervene in its efforts to keep California from counting ballots cast by individuals who it contends are noncitizens. Just hours later, the court denied the request. There were no noted dissents.

Later on Monday, the court released its calendar for the January argument session, which will begin on Monday, Jan. 11.

On Oct. 21, the Trump administration asked the court to allow it to resume construction of its border wall in the Big Bend region of southwestern Texas. The request has been fully briefed since last Friday, and the court’s decision could come at any time.

Looking ahead, the justices will hear arguments on Monday in two cases: Nielsen v. Watanabe, on whether and when federal officials can be held liable for constitutional violations; and Hoffmann v. WBI Energy Transmission, Inc., on whether the “just compensation” paid to land owners losing property to a pipeline project should be determined by referring to state law, which often provides for a higher payment than what the Constitution requires.

Jodi Kantor and Michael S. Schmidt, The New York Times

In May 2022, Politico released a leaked draft of the Supreme Court’s decision in Dobbs v. Jackson Women’s Health Organization, which overturned the right to abortion. The leak was “a seismic breach of confidentiality” and led to “an internal inquiry by the court,” although “no leaker has ever been publicly identified.” But The New York Times reported on Thursday that the Justice Department is now “pursuing a criminal investigation into who leaked” the draft opinion and that this “investigation has ramped up in recent weeks as prosecutors have sent grand jury subpoenas for testimony and sought information from people who worked at the court at the time of the leaks.”

Tuesday’s midterm elections “all but guaranteed” President Donald Trump won’t be able to put a fourth justice on the Supreme Court. Beginning in January, Democrats will have “a Senate majority—with a several-seat cushion—that will make it easy to block Trump judicial nominees, especially for any Supreme Court vacancies that might arise in 2027 or 2028,” according to The Wall Street Journal. Faced with this reality, “[s]ome legal observers have floated a last-ditch scenario in which [Clarence] Thomas or [Samuel] Alito could announce their retirement in the coming days and give Trump and the outgoing Republican Senate a chance to quickly install a successor before the newly elected Congress is seated on Jan. 3.” But the Journal described this scenario as “exceedingly unlikely,” in large part because neither Alito nor Thomas appear interested in retiring. And it’s not “clear that a Supreme Court nominee could get through the lame-duck Congress even if there were a retirement, especially because some moderate Republican senators are on their way out of office and blame Trump for losing their seats.”

In a Thursday interview with the Associated Press, Senate Democratic leader Chuck Schumer said “that Democrats will not allow President Donald Trump to put ‘a right-winger’ on the Supreme Court once they take power.” He “would not say if he would hold a Supreme Court vacancy open until after the next presidential election, as former Senate Republican leader Mitch McConnell did in 2016. But he said that Democrats are ‘not going to allow any crappy nominees’ to be confirmed to the Supreme Court.” “The bottom line is we’re not going to allow a right-winger on the court,” Schumer said.

In a column for Bloomberg, Noah Feldman contended that Tuesday’s election results “should cause Democrats to reconsider the wisdom of plans to pack the court or strip it of jurisdiction over certain issues.” It should no longer feel as urgent to make such changes, according to Feldman, because “[i]f a Democrat wins the presidency in 2028 and the Democrats hold the Senate, the Democrats will have a chance to flip the court from 6-3 conservative to 5-4 liberal” simply by filling predicted or expected vacancies. The reform proposals floated in recent months “make little sense if Democrats take seriously the prospect of a liberal recapture of the institution,” Feldman wrote.

The Supreme Court heard argument on Monday in Maxwell v. Dinis on whether a federal inmate may use a habeas statute to contest the Federal Bureau of Prisons’ refusal to accelerate his transfer to a halfway house or home confinement.

The argument on Nov. 10 in Department of Labor v. Sun Valley Orchards brings the justices a complex constitutional problem: Does the Constitution allow Congress to delegate to the executive branch – as opposed to the courts – the authority to adjudicate the obligation of an employer to comply with employment agreements it makes with temporary immigrant workers?

In his Empirical SCOTUS column, Adam Feldman offered an in-depth analysis of Justice Elena Kagan, examining not only how she votes, but also how she chooses to agree and disagree.

In her Ratio Decidendi column, Stephanie Barclay revisited Tuesday’s argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, noting that the discussion put a spotlight on 1990’s Employment Division v. Smith, a controversial ruling that made it easier for the government to overcome free exercise challenges. The ruling in St. Mary, according to Barclay, “will determine how much is left of” Smith, which has already fallen out of favor with the court.

In his Relist Watch column on Wednesday, John Elwood explored four petitions for review that will get a second look from the justices at today’s conference. Three concern a long-running dispute over Alabama’s congressional districts and the fourth asks whether using a car makes an otherwise local kidnapping a federal crime – even when the car never crosses a state line.

In his Courtly Observations column on Tuesday, Erwin Chemerinsky explored Nielsen v. Watanabe, in which the court is considering under what circumstances federal officers may be sued for money damages for constitutional violations, noting that the case comes at a time when efforts “to hold ICE agents accountable” have taken on new urgency.

On Oct. 19, the Supreme Court granted review in Association for Education Fairness v. Montgomery County Board of Education, a case on race and school admissions. In his AV Ristorante column on Monday, Brian Fitzpatrick explained why he believes “it could be the final nail in affirmative action’s coffin.”

Sarah Isgur and David French discuss the Texas Supreme Court’s blue wave, the possibility of a majority-Democrat Supreme Court, and one of the biggest Supreme Court cases of the term: St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy.

Friday’s A Closer Look is typically a judicial biography. This week we cover Justice Peter V. Daniel, who served on the court from 1841/2 to 1860.

Like some of the other justices we’ve covered, Justice Peter V. Daniel is one that “[f]ew recall or have ever heard of.” Those who know of Daniel have not been complimentary. He was called “a brooding proslavery fanatic” by law professor Earl Maltz, and a 1965 book review likened him to the notoriously bigoted Justice James C. McReynolds, minus McReynolds’ occasional “charm.”

Daniel was born in April 1784 on his family’s estate in Stafford County, Virginia. After studying with private tutors and a brief stint at the College of New Jersey (now Princeton University), he read law in Richmond under Edmund Randolph, President George Washington’s attorney general and secretary of state. In 1810, Daniel married Randolph’s daughter, Lucy.

In November 1808, Daniel fought a duel in Maryland against businessman John Seddon; Seddon subsequently died from his injuries. This did not appear to significantly slow Daniel’s career, however: He subsequently spent two one-year terms in the Virginia House of Delegates, a tenure his biographer, John P. Frank, judged as “utterly undistinguished.” In 1812, Daniel joined Virginia’s Council of State, which advised the governor, and from 1818 to 1835 served as lieutenant governor. His “friends” “apparently lacked any real warmth or affection for him, complaining of his ‘frozen deportment,’” which played out literally: In the winter of 1813, Daniel reportedly balked at spending a modest sum to keep militiamen warm, despite their officer’s warning that the men’s lives were at stake, on the reasoning that Virginia state law made the soldiers responsible for their own clothing.

Daniel’s Richmond law practice was modestly successful, but he had other aspirations. Through the Richmond Junto (a Virginia Democratic-Republican network) he organized and led the state’s Jacksonians, and, along with then-New York governor Martin Van Buren, built an “Albany-Richmond axis” which backed Andrew Jackson for president. Daniel subsequently turned down Jackson’s offer of attorney general. In 1836, however, he accepted a federal judgeship in eastern Virginia, Jackson’s reward for his party loyalty and his support for the president’s war on the national bank.

When Justice Philip Barbour died in February 1841, Van Buren had roughly a week left in office and rushed to nominate Daniel to the court. Aware of Daniel’s reputation, Whig senators tried to stall the vote, at one point walking out together to leave the chamber without a quorum, while Democratic leaders searched the capital for their own absent senators. Daniel was ultimately confirmed 22-5 in a midnight vote. The exact date he took the oath is unclear; the Federal Judicial Center lists it in January 1842 “based on questionable authority,” while one biography entry has him joining the court in December 1841.

After he joined the bench, Daniel’s circuit assignment included Arkansas and Mississippi, which involved arduous trips from Washington. While circuit riding in 1851, for example, Daniel rode in a canal boat for two days to get from D.C. to Pittsburgh (and was not a fan of the other “vulgar and filthy” passengers).

On the court itself, close to one in three of Daniel’s written opinions was a dissent, and during his tenure his solo dissents outnumbered any other justice’s by more than two to one. He fought against extending federal admiralty power to inland waters, and when the court did just that in 1852’s Genesee Chief v. Fitzhugh, Daniel was the lone dissenter. Perhaps his best-known majority opinion came in 1848’s West River Bridge Co. v. Dix, which put Daniel opposite the famed lawyer and statesman Daniel Webster. Daniel detested Webster so much that, after meeting him, he reported his hand “was actually contaminated by contact with his.” In the case, Vermont had taken over a privately chartered toll bridge, paid compensation, and opened it as a free road, but the bridge company argued that the takeover breached the contract clause of the Constitution. Writing for the majority, Daniel held that the franchise was ordinary property that the state could take through eminent domain.

Daniel, who was a slaveholder, concurred in 1842’s Prigg v. Pennsylvania, which held that the federal Fugitive Slave Act preempted Pennsylvania’s ban on recapturing formerly enslaved persons. After Van Buren joined the antislavery Free Soilers, Daniel’s friendship with him ended, and Daniel reportedly swore he would never again set foot north of Maryland (he came to blame even bad weather on the North). In Dred Scott v. Sandford, Daniel argued that freed slaves could not be citizens because they had once been held as property and that the Northwest Ordinance of 1787 (which, among many other things, had kept slavery out of land north and west of the Ohio River) was unconstitutional, thus taking a more extreme position than even Chief Justice Roger Taney, who wrote the majority opinion.

Daniel’s first wife, Lucy, died in 1847, and his second wife died in 1857 after a candle accidentally lit her clothes on fire. Daniel died in Richmond in May 1860 following a lengthy illness. In February 1861, Republican senators blocked President James Buchanan’s pick to replace him, Jeremiah Black, making the seat one of the three vacancies that President Abraham Lincoln filled in 1862. It stayed open until July 1862, when Lincoln chose Justice Samuel Miller.

In a December 1860 memorial, the attorney general said that Daniel “held the pen of a ready writer,” but “was sometimes in danger of going too directly to his purpose and treating with too much contempt the obstacles that intervened.”

JUSTICE THOMAS: “Does Atkins ever stop being a new rule?”

MR. SCHEPERS: “In our view, Atkins was new at the time it was decided.”

JUSTICE THOMAS: “We're talking about now, though.”

MR. SCHEPERS: “That – we believe that Atkins was new based off of the character of the decision in 2002 and that it remains new now.”

JUSTICE THOMAS: “What about Marbury v. Madison?”
